# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/).

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- **The server no longer goes dark while the index reconciles** (#413). Next awaits `register()` before dispatching a single request, and the in-process ingest watcher ran its full initial reconcile *inline* inside that hook. Until the whole corpus had been re-parsed, the server accepted connections and answered none of them — `/favicon.ico` and `/icon.svg` included, and with `MINDER_DEMO=1` set, where no backend should be touched at all. Measured at ~3 hours on a 6,078-session corpus (#431); reproduced here as 45-second timeouts on every route.

  **Two phases were blocking, not one.** Deferring the reconcile alone (`deferInitialReconcile: true`, mirroring the worker host's `awaitStart: false`) fixed the three-hour case and left a 30-second one: `chokidar`'s `ready` handshake walks the whole projects tree, and on this corpus it ran to its own timeout — with the server still dark for all of it. That was caught by re-running the probe rather than by reasoning, which is the only reason the first fix did not ship as complete. So ingest startup is no longer awaited at all: `startIngest()` launches the watcher and returns. Errors are still logged, just reported rather than waited on, and reconcile completion is reported through `onInitialReconcile` so a long re-parse after a `DERIVED_VERSION` bump stays visible instead of silent.

  **Watcher startup is serialized against itself**, which not awaiting made necessary. `startIngestWatcher` publishes its `globalThis` handle only *after* two awaits, so two entries landing in that gap would each arm a chokidar instance and leave the earlier one unreachable by `stopIngestWatcher()` — a duplicate reconcile writer that shutdown could never dispose. `register()` runs more than once under dev/HMR, which is how a second entry gets there. An in-flight promise guard (on `globalThis`, so it survives HMR, and cleared on settle so a later legitimate restart still works) collapses concurrent starts to one.

  **And cancelled against shutdown**, which not awaiting also made necessary. A signal can arrive while a detached start is still between `initDb()` and publishing its handle; the disposer's `stopIngestWatcher()` then finds nothing, SQLite closes behind it, and the start goes on to arm a watcher and reconcile into a closed database. `runBootstrap()` gates every one of its boot steps on `isShuttingDown()` for exactly this reason and the detached path had no equivalent. It now has three: the disposer drains the in-flight start (bounded at 2 s, so shutdown never inherits chokidar's 30 s handshake), the start re-checks `isShuttingDown()` after its dynamic import, and `startIngestWatcher` re-checks after its last await — and the drain clears its timer rather than leaving it armed, since `Promise.race` does not cancel the loser and a stray 2 s timer would hold the event loop open past the exit this disposer exists to make clean — the one that sits past every point a signal could straddle, which is what makes it the guarantee rather than a narrowing.

  **Three ways to reach the broken path**, all of them live before this: a source checkout (`next start` / `pnpm start`, where in-process is the default mode), the documented `MINDER_INDEXER_WORKER=0` opt-out, and — worst — the worker-start-failure fallback in `startIngest()`, which turned "the indexer worker could not start" into "the server serves nothing", precisely when something was already wrong. The packaged worker default from #431 masked the symptom for the common case; it never fixed it.

  **`MINDER_USE_DB=0` is not the cause**, contrary to the issue title it was filed under. The flag has no read site anywhere in the boot path — not at today's revision and not at the one the report was written against, where its only appearance in `instrumentation-node.ts` is a comment. Both of the originally reported arms serve correctly on a current build. What the one-variable bisect isolated instead was the ingest mode: with the in-process watcher every request hangs, and with `MINDER_INDEXER=0` on the same environment every request returns. A 1-second watchdog ticked right through the hang, which is how the loop was cleared of blocking synchronously and Next identified as holding requests behind an unresolved hook. The original flag correlation is not reproducible and is most likely a confounded two-run comparison; that is a limit of the evidence, not a claim about what the reporter did.

- **The file-parse backend no longer discards tool calls, and its tool numbers roughly double** (#453). Claude Code writes **one JSONL line per content block**, all sharing one `message.id`. Every assistant loop outside the SQLite index deduped by that id and `continue`d, which threw away each line after the first — and the later lines are the ones carrying `tool_use` blocks. Deduping *tokens* by `message.id` was always right (a re-logged message would double-count cost); discarding the *content* never was.

  Measured on this project's own transcripts, write-class edits only, one variable changed: **1,799 of 4,472 blocks before (40.2%), 4,470 of 4,472 after (99.96%)**. The 40.2% reproduces the figure in the issue exactly. The two blocks still dropped are genuine re-logs that repeat their `tool_use_id` — deleting the guard outright would read 4,472/4,472 and be quietly over-counting, so the residual is the evidence the safety property survived.

  Everything file-backed that reads `UsageTurn.toolCalls` moves with it: `topTools`, shell and MCP breakdowns, one-shot / retry detection, tool transitions and self-loops, and Hot Files / File Coupling under `MINDER_USE_DB=0`. These are **corrections, not regressions** — the index has been right since #426 and the two backends now agree.

  **Four copies of the defect, not the one the issue named.** `parser.ts` runs two near-identical assistant loops (`parseSessionTurns` and `parseSessionTurnsWithMeta`) that each carried it; `contextAttribution.ts` carried a third; `sessions/exportReader.ts` a fourth. Fixing one and missing its twin is precisely how this survived #426, so the parser merge now lives in one shared module (`src/lib/usage/assistantContinuation.ts`) that both loops call for the first line *and* every continuation — the continuation path cannot drift into a reduced copy that forgets tool-error lookup or slash-command matching.

- **Markdown session export no longer loses tool calls, and its "duplicates" count means what it says** (#453). `exportReader.ts` collapsed assistant lines by `message.id` and dropped every line after the first, so an exported transcript was missing the tool calls and any prose that arrived on a continuation line — and reported the loss as `duplicates`, which reads as "a re-log was tidied up for you" rather than "content is missing". Blocks are now merged into the message the id already owns, deduped at block level (on `tool_use_id` where present), and `duplicates` counts only lines whose every block was already there.

- **Per-turn context attribution counts thinking, prose and tool inputs that arrive on continuation lines** (#453). Under the same defect those bytes were dropped and then reported as `unattributedTokens` — the panel said "we cannot explain this part of your window" about content it had discarded. Affects both backends, since the report walks raw JSONL directly.

  `attributedAtPeak` is now derived from turn order at segment close rather than snapshotted the instant the peak was measured. A continuation can arrive long after its own first line — ingest measured gaps up to 3,639 lines — and if it lands after a *later* turn set the peak, its bytes still belong to a turn that preceded that peak, so they were in the window the peak measured. The live snapshot omitted them: on a fixture with one 8,000-byte tool input, `attributedAtPeak` read **2** where it should read **2,005**, with the whole difference landing in `unattributedTokens`. Where a line happens to sit in the file no longer changes the answer.

- **A `Skill` call split onto a continuation line is attributed to its own prompt.** The slash-command window is latched when a message opens rather than read live at merge time; a continuation can arrive after later user turns, and reading the cursor then would file the call as `auto` or, worse, credit it to an unrelated later slash command. Same defect Codex and Copilot caught on the ingest path in PR #427, avoided here rather than re-introduced in miniature.

  No `DERIVED_VERSION` bump: the index is untouched. This brings the file path *to* it.

## [1.12.1] - 2026-08-18

*A supply-chain release, and nothing else: no new surface, no behaviour you can see. Twenty of the twenty-three open Dependabot alerts close, and the framework pin that had been holding four of them open since v1.10.1 finally comes off.*

*The pin is the story. v1.10.0 upgraded to Next 16.3.0, which miscompiled an `async` function so that a value it provably returned reached its caller as `undefined` — four routes 500'd and v1.10.1 pinned back. That workaround cost four `postcss` alerts, and the trade was recorded rather than hidden. 16.3.1 does not exhibit the defect, established by running the gate **in both directions**: 16.3.0 was rebuilt and reproduced all four failures with their exact signatures, then 16.3.1 passed ten cold boots across two runs. Only the second half is the good news; the first half is what makes it mean anything, since a probe that has never been shown to fail cannot distinguish a fixed framework from a blind harness — which is exactly how the original upgrade shipped broken through a gate that passed.*

*Nobody found the root cause, so this is a result about one version rather than a reason to relax. The cold-boot gate still governs the next `next` bump, and the plan and TODO both say so in as many words.*

*No re-index — `DERIVED_VERSION` is unchanged at 20. Upgrading from 1.12.0 is a restart.*

### Changed

- **`next` un-pinned from `~16.2.12` to `~16.3.1`.** The pin was a workaround for a 16.3.0 defect that made `/api/usage`, `/api/stats`, `/api/skills` and `/api/sessions` return 500 on the first request after a cold boot — reliably enough to make all four pages unusable in practice, though a later request in the same warm process could succeed, which is exactly why the gate probes one cold boot per route: an `async` function in `src/lib/data/index.ts` returned an object that arrived at its caller as `undefined`. 16.3.1 does not exhibit it.

  Verified by the four-cold-boot gate recorded on #432, run **in both directions on the same machine with only the framework version differing**. 16.3.0 reproduced all four failures with the exact TypeErrors on record; 16.3.1 returned 200 on all five routes across two independent runs — ten cold boots, zero TypeErrors, with real payloads rather than 200-shaped errors. The negative-control arm is the load-bearing half: running only the candidate version cannot distinguish "fixed" from "the harness cannot see the bug," which is precisely how the original upgrade passed its gate and shipped broken.

  **The gate still governs future `next` bumps.** Nobody identified the root cause, so this is an empirical result about one version rather than a reason to trust the next one — and the two ways to make it go green while broken (a small index, or four probes after a single boot) are unchanged.

- `eslint-config-next` follows `next` to `~16.3.1`. The two ship as a lockstep pair, so leaving it behind would have had lint validating against the previous release's rules while the app built against the new one.

### Security

- **Sixteen alerts closed by refreshing vulnerable transitives** — `hono` 4.12.25 → **4.13.3** (7 alerts), `fast-uri` 3.1.2 → **3.1.5** (3), `ip-address` 10.2.0 → **10.5.0** (3), `@hono/node-server` 1.19.14 → **1.19.17**, `nanoid` 3.3.17 → **3.3.18**, and `body-parser` 2.2.2 → **2.3.0**.

  The root cause was one line: a `pnpm.overrides` entry pinning `hono` to `4.12.25`, added to pin the tree *up* to a patched version, which had since expired into pinning it *below* one. It accounted for seven alerts by itself. **The override was removed rather than re-pinned higher** — a fresh exact pin would expire into causing the same problem on the next advisory, and the MCP SDK's own range (`^4.11.4`) already admits the patched release, so nothing needed to be forced. No package upgrades were required; the lockfile simply had to be allowed to move.

  On exposure, honestly: nothing under `src/` imports `hono`, `express` or `body-parser` — the MCP SDK reaches them through transports this project does not use. `ajv` and `fast-uri` *are* live, so the JSON-RPC boundary was exercised against the rebuilt server with a real `initialize` handshake, which no unit test covers.

- **Closes the four `postcss` alerts (#396)**, which the `next` pin had held open since v1.10.1 — the lockfile moves off `8.4.31` to `8.5.23` + `8.5.26`.

  The `sharp` alert is **not** closed, and was never held by this pin — a correction to the earlier framing. 16.3.1 does move Next's own `sharp` to `0.35.3`, but the vulnerable `0.34.5` reaches the tree through `@huggingface/transformers`. Three alerts remain after this release: `sharp`, `glib` (in `Cargo.lock`), and `adm-zip` (via `onnxruntime-node`).

## [1.12.0] - 2026-08-18

*A tray-only release, and both entries are about the same subject: a server the tray did not start. 1.11.0 taught it to stop accusing its own still-booting server of being a stranger; this one closes the two remaining ways it could be wrong about a port it does not own — an update that destroys itself against a logon service holding the app's own files, and a "foreign" verdict that, once reached, was never revisited for the life of the tray.*

*Worth knowing how the first fix reaches you, because it is the unusual case where a release cannot repair the version installing it. The stop-before-install runs in the **installed** build, so 1.12.0 is the version that gains the ability, not the version the ability rescues: updating 1.11.0 → 1.12.0 still runs 1.11.0's updater, with the old failure intact if a logon service is pointed at the app's own bundle. Stop that service by hand for this one update if you run that configuration. From 1.12.0 onward the hand-off runs by itself.*

*No re-index — `DERIVED_VERSION` is unchanged at 20, verified by diffing against `v1.11.0` rather than assumed. Upgrading from 1.11.0 is a restart.*

### Fixed

- **A self-update no longer fails when the logon service is running the app's own bundle.** `--payload` (1.11.0) made it possible — and recommended — to point the Phase A logon service at the tray app's own server bundle, so both halves run the same build. But when that service owns the port the tray is in *attach* mode, where it deliberately never touches a server it doesn't own, including at quit. The two behaviors are each correct and together they broke updating: the service kept the app's own `resources/node/node.exe` open, and the Windows installer cannot overwrite a file a live process holds. On macOS and Linux the replacement succeeded while the service went on serving the **old payload from memory** — an update that reported success and changed nothing, which is the worse failure of the two.

  The updater now stops that service before handing off to the installer, but only on evidence: it reads the registration (`service-manifest.json`, the LaunchAgent plist, or the systemd unit) and acts only if it names a path *inside the app's own bundle*. A service running any other payload isn't holding these files and is left alone. The stop is delegated to the bundled `scripts/service.mjs` rather than reimplemented in Rust, because that script already carries the identity check — boundary-delimited matching against this installation's own entry point — that keeps `service:stop` from killing an unrelated `pnpm dev` sharing the port; re-deriving it would re-earn three rounds of review fixes in the one code path that runs once per update. It runs **after** the tray's own sidecar has stopped gracefully, since in spawn mode that sidecar answers to the same identity and would otherwise be hard-killed, forfeiting the clean-shutdown marker.

  It runs on both platform paths, which is not one line of code: the plugin's `on_before_exit` hook is **Windows-only** despite the neutral name — its sole call site sits inside the `cfg(windows)` installer branch, and the macOS and Linux implementations never invoke it — so the same stop is repeated on the post-install path those platforms actually reach. Getting that wrong would have left the POSIX half of the bug untouched while claiming it fixed, and it is the half that fails *quietly*.

  **The stop gates the install rather than being best-effort.** It runs *before a byte is downloaded*, so its failure aborts the update with a notification and leaves the user on a working version. Whether to run it is decided by comparing the port the registration declares against the tray's own, not by attach state: a service installed on its own port (`MINDER_PORT` at install time) runs entirely independently of whether the tray attached, and can be holding the bundle while the tray is happily spawning elsewhere. The single case that is skipped is the narrow one where our own sidecar bound the registered port — the service therefore cannot be running on it, and handing that port to the helper would find only our own child and hard-kill it. Every check the stop makes — the health probe that decides whether the port coming free is meaningful evidence, and the release wait itself — follows the **helper's** target port rather than the tray's, since in this configuration they are by definition different, and a compile-time tether pins the fallback default against `scripts/service/lib.mjs` so the two sides cannot drift into verifying different ports. A stop that merely logged its failure would reproduce the exact outcome being fixed. The evidence is weighed rather than assumed: a helper that reports failure is decisive, but a clean exit is not proof, because the helper deliberately exits 0 when it refuses to kill a process it cannot identify — so a clean exit is corroborated against the port, and only when a Minder was answering there to begin with (a foreign server holding the port says nothing about our service, and demanding it come free would abort a viable update). If the download then fails, the service is started again — but only if it was actually running when we stopped it, since the helper reports "nothing was listening" as success too and starting a service the user deliberately left down would be its own small betrayal. "Running" there means the port was **bound**, not that it answered: a service still opening a large index is bound and silent, which is the same distinction the tray fix above turns on, and keying this on a health response instead would have quietly dropped exactly those services on the floor. That debt is carried in a process-wide slot rather than by the update task, because the case it exists for is the task *not finishing*: Quit stays clickable through the download, and taking the process down mid-await would otherwise cost the user both the service and the tray's sidecar until their next logon, for an update that never happened. Claiming the slot is destructive, so the update task and the Quit handler can race it without ever starting the service twice.

  It deliberately does **not** restart the service after a *successful* install: the registration is logon-triggered on every platform, so it returns by itself, and starting it there would race the relaunching tray. An explicit "Check for updates…" that fails now says so, instead of looking identical to "already up to date". One visible consequence, documented in `docs/help/service-mode.md`: the stop is a hard kill, so the first launch after an update re-verifies the index.

- **A settled "foreign process" verdict now re-checks instead of latching for the life of the tray.** 1.11.0 fixed the case that actually bit — a still-booting Minder being called foreign — but left the mirror image: once a probe *proved* a non-Minder server held the port, the tray stopped asking forever. If that server then exited and Minder took the port, the 15-second health poll flipped the status word to "running" while the note stayed `port in use (foreign) — observing`, reproducing the same self-contradicting status line in a rarer path.

  The observe loop now re-probes a `Foreign` verdict at the slow end of the backoff (60s), on the reasoning that "foreign" is a claim about who holds the port *right now*, not a property of the port. Two rules keep that from thrashing, both pure functions with tests that fail when either is removed: a released port **voids** any verdict, because the owner it described is gone; and an inconclusive probe **never downgrades** a conclusive one, mirroring the existing rule that a timeout is not evidence of foreignness — it is equally not evidence against it. An attached Minder still blocks indefinitely and costs zero wakeups; that is the steady state, and a stale "attached to existing service" is at worst out of date rather than an accusation against our own server.

## [1.11.0] - 2026-08-17

*Mostly about Minder telling the truth about itself. A dashboard that reports on your work is worth only as much as its own honesty, and this cycle found several places where it was quietly wrong: demo mode serving real session data on seven surfaces, two charts that had never rendered for anyone on a default install, a search reporting "no matches" while matches existed, and a tray app that spent every boot accusing its own server of being a foreign process. The other half is speed — the panels that took 77 seconds now take half of one.*

*The tray fix has a root cause worth knowing about. `PRAGMA quick_check` runs on every database open and scales with file size, and because the SQLite driver is synchronous it blocks the entire server while it runs — measured at 2m47s on a 2.1 GB index read cold after a reboot, against 74ms warm. For that window the dashboard accepted connections and answered none, which is exactly what the tray was misreading as a foreign process. The check is now skipped after a provably clean shutdown, so orderly restarts are fast while reboots and force-kills are still verified.*

*No re-index — `DERIVED_VERSION` is unchanged at 20. Upgrading from 1.10.1 is a restart.*

### Fixed

- **The tray app no longer reports its own still-booting server as a foreign process.** After a reboot the tray showed `port in use (foreign) — observing` and never recovered; restarting it from the Start menu fixed things, which made it look like a startup race between the tray and the logon service. It wasn't. The port really was bound by Minder — the server just couldn't answer yet, and the tray had no way to say so.

  Two independent defects compounded. First, `health::probe` collapsed "a foreign server answered" and "nobody answered" into one `ServerStatus::Down`, so a 4-second timeout was treated as *proof* of a foreign owner. It isn't evidence of anything: the OS completes the TCP handshake from the listen backlog with no application code involved, so a port reads as bound while the process behind it is blocked. `Down` is now split into `Foreign` (something answered, and it wasn't Minder) and `Unreachable` (nothing answered), and only the former settles the question.

  Second, the verdict was a one-shot decision. `decide_mode` ran once, synchronously, at `Supervisor::start`; attach mode then blocked on `rx.recv()` forever, so the 15-second health poll refreshed the status *word* while the stale note stayed pinned — rendering the self-contradicting `Status: running (:4100) — port in use (foreign) — observing`. An unsettled verdict is now re-probed on a 2s→60s backoff that **never expires**: an earlier design lapsed a grace period and then declared "foreign", which just relocated the same bug, since a server slower than the window would be mislabelled permanently. Waiting longer is free; being confidently wrong is not. The pending note says `port bound, not responding — observing` and claims nothing a timeout can't support.

  The crash path got the same treatment. When our sidecar loses the EADDRINUSE race to the logon service, that service is by definition mid-bootstrap and cannot answer a probe — so `decide_after_crash` previously mislabelled the exact process that had just beaten it to the port.

- **A large SQLite index no longer makes the server unreachable for minutes after a reboot.** `initDb()` ran `PRAGMA quick_check` on every open. The scan is O(database size) and better-sqlite3 is synchronous, so it blocks the Node event loop outright — every in-flight request stalls, `/api/health` included, which is what starved the tray's probe above. Measured on a real 2.1 GB `~/.minder/index.db`: **2m 47s** from a cold page cache after a reboot, against **74 ms** for the same call warm. For those ~3 minutes the dashboard accepted connections and answered none.

  The check now runs unless the previous shutdown was provably clean. Proof is deliberately over-determined: a marker written by `checkpointAndCloseDb()` **only after the WAL is confirmed drained** (the checkpoint pragma reports a busy failure by returning, not by throwing, so "the call didn't throw" would have certified dirty databases as clean), plus a size+mtime binding that any subsequent write invalidates, plus an independent check that the `-wal` sidecar is empty. Indexes under 256 MB always get checked, since the scan is milliseconds there and skipping it would trade real corruption detection for nothing measurable. Reboots, force-kills and power losses therefore still get verified — only orderly restarts skip ahead. `MINDER_FORCE_QUICK_CHECK=1` forces a full scan.

  The marker is **not** consumed on read, because two processes open this index at boot — the server and the ingest worker. Consume-on-read would hand the fast path to whichever opened first and leave the other grinding through the full scan for no added safety. Invalidation comes from the file changing, not from someone having looked. `db: probed` now logs elapsed `ms`, so a recurrence is one grep away instead of an inference from timestamp gaps.

- **`service:install` can target a server payload outside the repo.** It previously resolved only `<root>/dist/minder-server`, which the installed desktop app's bundle (a flatter `minder-server/` beside its own `node/`) can't satisfy. A machine with both installed therefore ran *different builds* depending on which won the boot — the app's bundle from the Start menu, the dev tree's at logon — turning the port collision into a question of which version you got. `--payload <dir>` (or `MINDER_SERVICE_PAYLOAD`) points the service at a specific bundle, preferring a Node runtime shipped beside it so the service stays on the version that bundle was built against. A named directory without `server.js` is a hard error rather than a silent fall-back to the repo build, since installing a payload the user didn't ask for is the failure being fixed. Reported as mode `standalone`, so the existing identity-marker and vbs-recovery paths that `service:stop` depends on keep working unchanged.

- **Demo mode no longer serves real data on seven surfaces.** An import-graph audit of all 165 API routes (`docs/demo-mode-coverage.md`) replaced the previously-suspected four instances. Now fixture-backed under `MINDER_DEMO=1` / the `demoMode` flag: the `/stats` cross-check panel (which published the user's real session and message totals, and blocked a clean screenshot pass on its own), the workflow catalog (#441 — names, run counts, session ids and absolute script paths), `/api/plans` and `/api/plans/[slug]` (plan titles and bodies), `/api/adapters/[id]/config` (the harness config home and the full text of its rules files), `/api/claude-config` (user-scope hook commands, MCP servers, the installed plugin list), and the app shell's `devRoot`, which appeared in the top bar of *every* screen because `RootLayout` is a server component sitting outside both the façade and any route.

  Guards were placed at the shared loader each surface calls, not in the route handlers, so the seam stays closed when the next caller appears. Two corrections to the prior record: **`/api/adapters` was never a leak** (it returns the code-defined adapter registry), and the audit's own "guarded" count is unreliable — reaching a `demoMode()` call through the import graph is not the same as being guarded by one, which is how `/api/claude-config` was previously classified as covered.

- **Toggling demo mode in Settings now clears the route caches.** `/api/plans` and `/api/claude-config` cache their responses under keys that carry no mode (`"all"`, `type|project`), and the cache is read *before* the guarded loader — so an entry warmed in real mode kept serving real plan titles and hook commands for the rest of its 2-minute TTL after the toggle, straight past a correct guard. `PATCH /api/config` now calls `disposeAllRouteCaches()`, which covers both routes, the demo→real direction (fixtures lingering into real mode), and every future `getOrCreateRouteCache` caller. Only ever affected the Settings-flag path; `MINDER_DEMO=1` is set before boot, so no real-mode entry exists to serve.

- **Demo mode no longer probes MCP servers at boot.** `GET /api/mcp-health` was already fixture-backed, but `bootMcpHealthCache()` calls `enqueueMcpHealth()` directly, bypassing the route. With the new user-config fixtures in place that path would have sent a real reachability request to the fixture's Linear SSE endpoint — and, with the opt-in `mcpHealthStdioProbe` flag, executed `npx -y @modelcontextprotocol/server-github`, downloading packages. Demo mode is meant to be inert, so the enqueue helper now returns the server list without probing it.

- **The demo stats chart no longer contradicts its own headline.** `dailyActivity()` floored `sessionCount` at 1 unconditionally, so a fixture with 0 sessions rendered 14 days of activity under a total reading 0. The floor now applies only when sessions exist.

- **Hot Files and File Coupling are served from the SQLite index instead of a portfolio-wide JSONL parse.** Both routes ran `parseAllSessions()` — parsing *every* session in the portfolio — and then filtered down to the one project being viewed, so their cost scaled with total history rather than with the project. Measured on one production build, same data, backend flipped with `MINDER_USE_DB=0`: File Coupling went from **77.1s to 0.48s** (~160x). Hot Files went from 177.7s to 45.3s, where the remaining 45s is the cold `scanAllProjects()` sweep every route shares, not this one's work. (#439)

  The aggregators were split so both backends share them (`buildHotFilesFromEdits` / `buildFileCouplingFromEdits`), and project scoping reuses the file path's own `projectDirNameCandidates` rather than a slug comparison — sessions recorded under a mapped path (a WSL checkout of the same repo) match on dirname, not slug, so a naive `WHERE project_slug = ?` would have returned an empty panel. Responses now carry `X-Minder-Backend: db|file`, so which path served a request is observable rather than inferred from timing.

- **The two panels now report substantially more activity, and the new number is the correct one.** On the reference project they go from 1,651 to 4,164 write edits — because the file-parse path silently drops `tool_use` blocks. Claude Code writes one JSONL line per content block sharing a `message.id`, and `parser.ts` skips repeated lines wholesale, keeping only the first block. Verified against raw transcripts: 4,164 blocks on disk, 4,164 in the index, 1,651 from the parse. This is the #426 defect, fixed in ingest and still live in the file path; tracked as **#453**, which also affects tool counts, MCP and shell stats, and one-shot detection under `MINDER_USE_DB=0`.

- **The Tool Execution Flow chart now has data on the default backend.** `usageFromDb.ts` returned `[]` for `toolTransitions` and `toolSelfLoops`, and the DB backend is the default, so the chart's own render gate (`length > 0`) meant it had **never appeared for any user on a normal install** — measured against a real index, 99,053 turns over 90 days produced zero transitions. The file-parse path computed them correctly the whole time, so this was a backend divergence rather than a missing feature. (#450)

  The SQL path **reuses `computeToolTransitions`** instead of expressing the pairing in SQL a second time. The definition is fiddly — consecutive calls inside a turn, plus the last tool of one turn to the first of the next, per session, with tool-less turns not breaking the chain — and a second implementation would have been free to drift while both kept returning a valid shape. That is the divergence class found in `byCategory.oneShotRate`; one definition, two callers. Ordering comes from `(turn_index, sequence_in_turn)`, the transcript's own order, which `tool_uses` already stores as its primary key — so no schema change and no re-index.

  On this machine's index the chart now draws 200 transitions and 70 self-loops, led by `Edit → Bash` (4,607) and `Bash → Edit` (3,232) — **a cycle in the top two edges**, which is why the acyclic projection shipped first: without it this change would have taken `/usage` down on real data rather than fixing it.

- **`/usage` no longer takes the whole page down when the tool-flow graph contains a cycle.** The Tool Execution Flow Sankey fed its transitions straight to d3-sankey, which requires a DAG and throws `Error: circular link` on anything else. That throw happened inside a `useMemo` during render, so it was not a broken chart — the route died and the browser replaced it with its own "This page couldn't load" screen. Reproducible under `MINDER_DEMO=1`, which is the mode meant for first-run and screenshots; a capture run had already written a 2880×1800 PNG of the browser error page, and every guard passed because navigation resolved and the file was the right size. (#443)

  The data is **inherently cyclic** — transitions are consecutive tool pairs, and `Edit → Bash → Edit` is an ordinary workflow — so the fix is an acyclic projection rather than an assumption that the data behaves. The lowest-volume transition in each cycle is dropped, and **the chart says how many were hidden**, with the specific transitions in an expandable disclosure next to it: a Sankey quietly missing edges reads as a complete picture of the flow. The disclosure is a `<details>` rather than a hover tooltip, so it is reachable by keyboard and on touch. A `try`/`catch` around the layout is the second line of defence, so any future d3-sankey refusal degrades to one unrendered chart instead of a dead route.

  Self-loops are excluded silently rather than counted as hidden, since they are already drawn elsewhere on the same chart.

- **A filtered session search no longer reports "no sessions" when matches exist.** The starred / source / entrypoint filters were applied in the browser, *after* `/api/sessions/search` had already returned its globally ranked top 200. Any query matching more than 200 sessions could therefore show an empty list for a filter that genuinely had matches — they simply all ranked below the cut. The failure was silent and indistinguishable from a correct empty result, and it was reachable in ordinary use: a common word easily exceeds 200 hits, and deep-transcript matches are exactly what FTS exists to find. All three filters are now applied **in SQL, inside every retriever**, so the limit counts filtered rows. (#425)

  `GET /api/sessions/search` accordingly accepts `source`, `entrypoint` and `starred`. **Empty** values are rejected with a 400 on all three (`?source=` more likely means a client built the URL wrong than that it wanted every source), and `starred` additionally rejects anything other than `1`/`true` rather than coercing — reading `?starred=0` as "starred" would answer a different question than the one asked, silently. `source` and `entrypoint` deliberately accept **any** non-empty value: both are open sets that grow when an adapter or entrypoint is added, so an allowlist would reject a legitimate new value, and an unknown one already returns an empty result that is *true* rather than misleading.

  All three filters were fixed together rather than one at a time: they sit in one visually identical block, so leaving two on the old behaviour would have been the "identical paths, only one guarded" shape that produced the bug. The same reasoning applies inside the fix — the title retriever's seven-column `OR` chain had to be parenthesized before a filter could be `AND`ed to it, or six of the seven columns would have ignored the filter while looking guarded.

- **A re-index no longer takes the dashboard down with it.** The packaged server now hosts ingest in a worker thread by default. `better-sqlite3` is synchronous, so a reconcile on the main thread monopolizes the event loop and the HTTP server accepts connections it never reads — after the 1.10.0 `DERIVED_VERSION` bump that meant **~3 hours of total unavailability** on a 6,078-session history, every route timing out including static ones, with the process pinned at ~101% of one core. Worker mode is what `workerHost.ts` was built for ("the HTTP server stays up"); it had simply never been switched on for the packaged path, whose `server.js` set only `MINDER_SERVER_ROOT`. (#431)

  Both opt-outs keep working: `MINDER_INDEXER_WORKER=0` is honoured from the environment *or* `.env.local`, and `MINDER_INDEXER=0` still disables automatic index updates entirely — it takes precedence, so the worker cannot re-enable ingest you turned off. A worker that fails to spawn falls back to the in-process watcher, so ingest cannot end up off unintentionally either.

- **Packaging now fails loudly if the ingest worker bundle is missing**, rather than warning and shipping a package that silently degrades back to the blocking in-process path. The generated `server.js` is also asserted to carry the worker default, so a future edit to that template can't quietly drop the fix.

- **A repeatedly-crashing ingest worker now hands off instead of leaving the index frozen.** On exhausting its crash budget (5 non-zero exits in an hour) the host gave up without telling the caller, whose `startIngest()` had returned long before — so nothing restarted ingest and the SQL-backed views quietly went stale until the server was restarted. That was survivable while worker mode was a developer opt-in; with the packaged server now defaulting to it, the same path would strand an install with no ingest at all. The host now invokes the failure callback, which starts the in-process watcher. Related: the callback is registered unconditionally rather than only when a start handshake is sent, so it can no longer be silently dropped.

## [1.10.1] - 2026-08-11

*A one-line dependency revert, and nothing else.* Four of the app's main pages returned 500 on every request in 1.10.0 — `/usage`, `/stats`, `/skills` and `/sessions` — on a healthy, fully-indexed database. The cause was not in this codebase: **Next.js 16.3.0** miscompiles an `async` function in the read-side data façade so that a value the function provably returns arrives at its caller as `undefined`. Pinning back to 16.2.12 restores all four.

> ### ⚠️ This re-opens four Dependabot alerts
>
> The 16.3.0 upgrade closed four postcss advisories (#396). Reverting the version brings them back. That is a deliberate trade: a transitive dev-time advisory is a smaller problem than four broken pages, and the alerts return the moment a fixed Next release lands.

### Fixed

- **`/api/usage`, `/api/stats`, `/api/skills` and `/api/sessions` no longer 500.** Bisected to `7d96242` (the Next.js 16.3.0 bump) against an isolated copy of a real 6,078-session index, then isolated further by taking that commit's *parent* and changing only the Next version — same application code, same config, same database, opposite result. Under 16.3.0 the façade logs `getUsage returning object [ 'report', 'meta' ]` while the route logs `getUsage returned undefined` for the same call. Reproduces on the first request after a cold boot against a large index; later requests in the same process sometimes succeed, which is why it reads as flaky rather than broken. `/api/agents` was never affected and served as the control throughout. (#432)

### Changed

- **Next.js 16.3.0 → 16.2.12**, and `eslint-config-next` with it. The payload-hygiene work that shipped alongside the upgrade — the `outputFileTracingExcludes` entries keeping `.env*`, `.mcp.json` and `.minder.json` out of the traced payload — is **kept**, since it is independent of the framework version.

## [1.10.0] - 2026-08-11

*Two features, and a sweep through numbers that were quietly wrong.* The additions are a **billable-hours timecard** and an **entrypoint filter** that separates two populations of session differing 61× in cost. The substance, though, is corrective: the index was storing roughly a quarter of the tool calls it saw, the delegation-cap warning could not see subagent work at all, and long-context bills were priced at standard rates. Each was invisible for the same reason — the system reporting the number could not represent the data it was missing, so every query against it confirmed the wrong answer. Node 20 support is removed.

**Upgrading re-indexes your history.** The derivation version is now 20; any session below it is re-parsed in the background — from 17 on a typical existing install, budget about an hour for ~6,000 sessions. Until it completes, tool counts read low and the delegation-cap chip does not appear. Nothing shown is wrong; there is just less of it.

### Added
- **An entrypoint filter on `/sessions`.** Sessions already carried an entrypoint chip, but there was no way to look at one population on its own — and the two populations barely resemble each other. Re-measured on the current index: an interactive (`cli`) session costs **$13.43** against an SDK-driven one's **$0.22**, roughly **61×**, while the volumes run the other way — **69%** of the 6,024 sessions are SDK-driven and account for **4%** of the spend. Any impression formed from the unfiltered list is an impression of a mixture no individual session resembles. The control offers Interactive / SDK (CLI) / SDK (Python) / Unknown, appears only when the loaded sessions contain more than one entrypoint, and filters on the same bucketing the rest of the app uses so an empty-string entrypoint is reachable under **Unknown** rather than being unselectable.

- **Timecard: how much of an agent session you actually attended, in billable hours.** New `/timecard` route, a per-project **Timecard** tab, `GET /api/engagement`, and a CSV/JSON export at `GET /api/engagement/export`. Gated by the default-on `engagementReport` flag. The problem it solves is that transcript span is not effort: an agent that ran unwatched for two hours leaves the same footprint as two hours of supervised pair-work, and consulting invoices need to tell those apart.

  The discriminator is **not** the gap between your prompts, which is the intuitive metric and the wrong one — supervising a 40-minute agent run produces a 40-minute gap with you watching throughout, while a 2-minute run you walked away from produces a 3-hour gap. The gap can't separate them; how fast you replied *once the agent fell silent* can. So each inter-prompt gap is split at the agent's last event into `agentBusy` and `quiet`, and the gap is credited (`min(agentBusy, runCap) + quiet`) only when `quiet` is inside the idle threshold. Agent work inside an unattended gap earns nothing but tail credit — deliberately the conservative reading, since on a client invoice under-billing is the smaller professional risk.

  All three thresholds were measured on the reference index (120 sessions / 14,334 turns / 579 latency samples), not chosen for roundness. The response-latency distribution decays smoothly, but its **per-minute density** falls off a cliff between 10–15 min (1.08 %/min) and 15–20 min (0.32 %/min) — a 3.4× drop where replying stops and a flat "walked away" tail begins. Hence a **15-minute** default rather than the 10 that intuition suggests, which sits mid-slope and clips ~9 % of genuine replies. The agent-run cap is **30 min** (p95 of observed agent-busy spans; p50 2.7, p90 18.1, max 512) and tail credit **3 min**. All three are live sliders, because the honest answer to "is 15 right?" is to let the operator watch the number move: 5 min yields 45.6 h on the reference project where 30 min yields 65.0 h.

  Two findings shaped the implementation more than the formula did. **Automated sessions are excluded structurally**, via `entrypoint LIKE 'sdk-%'`, not by matching prose: a full-corpus audit found 7,493 plain-text `user` turns portfolio-wide of which ~63 % were scripted agent invocations, 3,052 of them a single cron-driven project's `"You are the staff historian for ..."` prompt, which no text heuristic separates from human writing. Without that clause the project books ~24 h of phantom attended time. And **per-project hours are de-overlapped**: 30 % of attended time portfolio-wide (42.6 % on the reference project) overlaps another repo, so a naive per-project sum double-bills. Concurrent instants are split evenly between the active projects, which makes per-project hours sum exactly to the day's total. Both raw and billable figures are shown so the discount is visible rather than mysterious.

  Day buckets follow the browser's timezone rather than UTC, and the CSV carries a provenance block (period, timezone, all three thresholds, overlap discount) — a billable figure a client can question should travel with the definition that produced it. SQL-only: with `MINDER_USE_DB=0` the route returns an explicit 503 rather than an empty report that would read as "you did no billable work".

  Review (PR #418) found four defects in the first cut that share one shape — **arithmetic that reconciled while the instants underneath it were wrong**. Credit was accumulated forward from each block's start, so after any capped gap every later interval was recorded earlier than it happened; block totals stayed right, which is exactly why it looked correct, but day bucketing and overlap allocation read real instants, so an evening exchange after a long capped run could book on the wrong calendar day or miss an overlap entirely. Credit is now anchored to end at the reply that earned it — a capped run credits its final 30 minutes, not its first. Alongside that: `today` derived its cutoff from *server* midnight while bucketing rows in the *viewer's* zone, so the window and its own rows disagreed off-UTC; an attended gap straddling the period boundary lost the work after the boundary, because cutting its opening prompt left the remainder unrecognizable as attended (the query now over-fetches 24 h and clips); and per-project day shares were each rounded independently, so small concurrent slices displayed a sum that contradicted the day total the report promises they add up to — shares are now apportioned by largest remainder, making all three reconciliation invariants exact. Two homes with identical path layouts also merged into one row, so project identity now carries the `home_key` discriminator like the usage report's does (#311).

  A second review round found the same class again, one level up. **Attendance was judged across a project's whole turn stream rather than within a session**: if one session's agent was still working when a *different* session's opening prompt arrived, the merged walk read that prompt as a reply and credited the unwatched run as supervised. Running a main checkout beside a worktree is the normal case here, so this was routine rather than exotic. Blocks are now built per session and their intervals unioned per project — union, not sum, so two genuinely concurrent sessions still bill their shared minutes once. Alongside it: tail credit could run past the moment the report was generated, minting future minutes and, on a `today` report, a row dated tomorrow (credited time is now clipped at both ends); a block straddling the lower boundary kept its full prompt count, including prompts from the over-fetched lead-in; the CSV discarded `homeKey`, producing duplicate indistinguishable rows in the one artifact that gets invoiced (it now carries a `home` column); and the dashboard's name lookup was keyed on the bare usage slug, so two homes rendered under one project's name. Finally, **apportioning days and projects as two independent one-dimensional problems does not converge** — `apportionRounded` can award at most one extra hundredth per share, so a project earning ~0.0556 h on each of 30 days accumulated 1.80 h in the daily table against a 1.67 h period share, and the project table stopped adding up to the headline. Project totals are now summed from the reconciled daily matrix, which is the only arrangement where every margin agrees; verified on live data at 168.74 h with 0 of 31 days out of balance.

  A third round caught the one remaining way to bill time that never happened: **the query took every adapter's sessions, and not every adapter records a timestamp per turn.** The Codex adapter stamps each turn with the session's creation time by its own admission, which collapses a whole conversation to a single instant — so each of its sessions would have contributed a zero-length block plus the flat tail credit, as phantom hours, merged into any Claude project sharing the path, and its null entrypoint meant the automated-session filter did not catch it. Latent rather than live (this index holds no adapter sessions), but a billing surface should not wait for the configuration that makes it real: the report is now restricted to sources with genuine per-turn timestamps, and being timestamp-poor is treated as a reason to be excluded rather than approximated. Two smaller ones alongside: prompt counts were folded into the billable-interval branch, so with tail credit at zero an isolated prompt reported as no prompts at all; and `activeDays` counted the display-rounded rows, so a day whose share rounded to `0.00` stopped counting as a day with attended time.

### Changed
- **Next.js 16.2.12 → 16.3.0.** Taken as its own change rather than inside a dependency chore, so a broken installer build is attributable. It closes the last four Dependabot alerts from #396: Next pins `postcss` *exactly* rather than with a caret, which welded those alerts to the framework version — a `pnpm.overrides` entry would have resolved a `postcss` Next never tested against, so upgrading was the only reachable fix. 16.3.0 pins 8.5.23 against 16.2.12's 8.4.31. No configuration changes were required and the suite is unchanged at 4,741 passing.

- **The usage help page describes the period filters the app actually has.** It still documented calendar-aligned "This Week (Sunday)" and "This Month (1st)" options that were replaced by rolling windows, and listed four periods where there are now seven (24h / Today / 7d / 30d / 90d / 1y / All time). The rewrite states the rolling-window behaviour explicitly — the same period can total differently minutes later as work ages out — notes that **Today** is the one calendar-aligned option, and documents the `week`/`month`/`quarter`/`year` legacy aliases, including that a bookmarked link now resolves to a slightly different span than when it was saved. Fixes #188.

### Removed
- **Node 20 is no longer supported — the minimum is now Node 22.12.** `engines.node` goes from `^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0` to `>=22.12.0`, and the CI matrix, `release.yml`, `pr-review-responder.yml`, and `package-standalone.mjs`'s `EXPECTED_NODE_ENGINES` follow. Two reasons, one forcing and one overdue. The forcing one: `claude-code-lint` 0.7.x depends on `markdownlint` `^0.41.0`, and markdownlint raised its floor to `node >=22` at 0.41.0 — so the config-lint CLI, which is spawned with the app's own `process.execPath`, could no longer be honestly claimed to run on Node 20. The overdue one: **Node 20 reached end-of-life on 2026-04-30** and has received no security patches since, so the previous range advertised support for an unpatched runtime. Windows CI had already been pinned to Node 22 for an unrelated reason (better-sqlite3 ships no win32-x64 prebuilt for Node 20's ABI 115 — issue #264), so in practice only the ubuntu job still exercised Node 20. Note this class of breakage is invisible to the test suite: the real linter CLI is mocked, so the Node 20 job passed green while the spawned binary would have been the thing to fail.

### Fixed
- **The delegation-cap warning was blind to everything a subagent did — which is where most of the delegation happens.** Claude Code caps subagent spawns and web searches **per session**, but the chip on `/sessions` compared those caps against counts of what the *root* turn set did. Work delegated to a subagent spends the same budget and was invisible, so the chip stayed silent in precisely the runaway case it exists to catch. Measured on the reference history: **123 of 165 web searches** were made inside subagents, i.e. the visible count was under a third of the real one.

  The reason it was invisible is more specific than an oversight. Modern Claude Code writes a subagent's turns to its own file at `<project>/<session-id>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl`, which Minder indexes as a **separate session** — no shared id, and `turns.parent_tool_use_id` NULL on all 188,181 rows. On top of that, a subagent's tool calls were stored nowhere at all: `tool_uses` has only ever held primary turns. So the read side queried a table that structurally could not answer and took the resulting zero as "no nested delegation". A zero from a system is not evidence of absence until you have checked the system can represent a non-zero — and the issue, the plan and the code comments had all been written from that zero.

  The fix adds the missing fact rather than widening the ones already on screen: a `sidechain_tool_uses` table recording each subagent tool call, keyed on its `tool_use_id` so repeated indexing passes cannot double it. Parent linkage is derived from the transcript's own path — the only linkage this data has — at read time rather than stamped on the session row, because a stored column would be written by the same parse that marks a session re-derived, leaving exactly the stale children the completeness check exists to catch invisible to it. `tool_uses` is deliberately left alone: around twenty queries behind `/usage`, `/agents`, `/skills`, `/costs` and the denial analytics read it with no sidechain predicate, and adding subagent rows would have moved every one of those numbers at once. The chip now reads a new `treeDelegation` field summing the root and every transcript below it; **Subagents**, the per-session tool tables and all cost reporting keep their existing "this session's own calls" meaning.

  **The chip disappears until your index re-derives, and that is the intended behaviour.** The roll-up reports itself unmeasured unless the session *and every subagent transcript below it* have been re-parsed at the new derivation version — a partial tree total would understate by exactly the nested work the chip exists to surface, which is the original bug wearing a new field's name. For the same reason it does not appear at all under `MINDER_USE_DB=0`, which reads transcripts directly and never opens the `subagents/` directory. One residual gap remains and is one-directional: a subagent transcript that was never indexed is invisible, so a shown total can still under-count — it cannot over-count, which matters for a badge that asserts a session may have been silently truncated.

  Fixes #395. Split out of the #388 review, where it was assessed and deliberately deferred as needing a sidechain-aware session tree rather than a call-site change.

- **The index was storing a fraction of your tool calls — 94% of sessions had none recorded at all.** Claude Code writes **one JSONL line per content block**, so a message that thought twice and then called two tools is four lines sharing a `message.id`, each repeating the message-level token usage verbatim. Ingest treated any repeat of that id as a re-logged message and skipped the line — throwing away whatever block it carried. Deduplicating the *tokens* is right; discarding the *entry* is what lost the tools, because the surviving line is usually the text-only one.

  The scale only becomes visible against the raw transcripts. On one 47 MB session the file contains 2,716 `tool_use` blocks and the index held **720** — exactly those sitting on a first-seen id — with `Agent` spawns at **72 → 6**, `Edit` at 853 → 312, and `Bash` at 573 → 86. Across the whole index, **5,652 of 6,036 sessions had no `tool_uses` rows whatsoever**, and root-session subagent spawns read 87 against 1,273 actually present. Six sampled sessions with zero rows turned out to have *100%* of their tool blocks on continuation lines, so the empty ones were the same bug rather than a gap in coverage. The file-parse backend has no such deduplication and reported 72 for that session against the index's 6 — a 12× disagreement that three parity tests ran green across, because every existing fixture puts a message on one line and so cannot express the shape that breaks.

  Blocks are now unioned into the turn their message already owns, deduplicated on `tool_use_id` and on block body so the genuine re-log the guard was written for is still dropped (22 such duplicates exist among 5,591 blocks here). Tokens remain counted once — every continuation line carried identical usage, 3,248 of them with none differing, so summing per line would have multiplied a message's cost by its block count. The merge keys on `message.id` rather than on the preceding turn, since continuations are usually adjacent but not reliably so: one measured gap ran 3,639 lines. A late continuation cannot revive its turn's pending tool calls and so cannot flip a finished session back to "needs attention".

  Text was affected the same way and is fixed with it: a message whose first block was `thinking` stored **no prose at all**, truncating the turn preview, the full-text search body, and the assistant text the classifier reads. Because that text and those tool calls are classifier inputs, categories — and the one-shot rates derived from them — move once the index rebuilds.

  This is why the delegation badge could stay silent on heavily-delegating sessions (#395): the count it compares against the cap was missing 93% of the spawns. **`DERIVED_VERSION` 18 → 19**, so existing indexes re-parse; until that completes, stored rows hold the old short counts, and nothing about them looks broken from the read side. Fixes #426.

- **The transcript half of Hook Activity was unreachable from the UI, and now has a source toggle.** The card asked for `auto`, which prefers OTEL and falls back only when OTEL returns nothing — but the time window is a *lower* bound, so every period ending at now includes recent events and no choice of period, including **All time**, ever triggers the fallback. Once telemetry had produced a single row the transcript pipeline could not be reached from any control on the page. On this machine that left 20.8k transcript-derived runs, keyed by the *command* each hook ran, invisible behind 81.1k OTEL records keyed by *hook name*. An **Auto / OTEL / Transcript** control now sits on the card, `GET /api/telemetry/hook-activity` accepts `?source=`, and an unrecognized value is a 400 rather than a silent fallback to `auto` — the two pipelines count different things, so substituting one for the other answers a question nobody asked. Asking for a source that has no data reports that honestly and names the source, and the toggle stays on screen in the empty state so the choice is reversible. The MCP half (`get-hook-activity(source:)`) shipped in 2026-08-07; the help page's claim that it was "the only way" to read transcript runs is now retired.

- **Per-category one-shot rates were structurally unable to see an ordinary task, and are now reported on both backends.** `byCategory.oneShotRate` was computed by slicing each category's turns out of the session and re-running the one-shot detector over the slice. That cannot work: the classifier files the edit under `Coding` and the `pnpm test` that judges it under `Testing`, so every ordinary task was split across two slices — an edit with no verification in one, a verification with no edit in the other — and neither half formed a task. The only tasks that survived were the ones whose edit and verification happened to classify identically, which in practice means test-file edits verified by a test command. The symptom was quiet rather than wrong-looking: the headline counted the task normally while every category reported no rate at all, so the field read as "not measured yet" instead of "measured incorrectly". No test pinned the behaviour.

  Each task is now attributed to the category of the turn that *started* it, matching what `byEffort` and `bySkill` already do and what the `turns.task_outcome` column already records. Because that definition is a plain `GROUP BY` on the SQL side, it also closes the long-standing divergence where the DB backend left the field undefined entirely (documented in `usageFromDb.ts` since P2b-2) — the two backends are now pinned to agree by test rather than by intent. On the reference index this turns 5 of 13 categories from "no rate" into a real figure over 1,699 verified tasks, and the figures differ enough to be worth having: `Refactoring` first-passes at 92.1% against `Debugging`'s 78.3%. Read-time only — no re-ingest, no `DERIVED_VERSION` bump.

- **Long-context pricing now covers the cache rates, which are most of a long-context bill.** The above-200K tier is a whole-*request* tier, and cached tokens are part of the request — but only the input and output rates switched to it; cache read and cache write stayed at base. That is the wrong two-thirds on exactly the requests the tier exists for: a real long-context Claude Code turn arrives as roughly 5K new input against 220K cache read, so ~98% of the prompt was billed at half rate. Cache read, 5-minute cache write and 1-hour cache write now each carry their own above-200K rate, and a **Cache Read $/M** or **Cache Write $/M** override scales the tiered rate by the same proportion it scales the base one, as the input and output overrides already did. Where a model publishes a tiered 5-minute write rate but no tiered 1-hour one — LiteLLM's dated Sonnet 4 entries do exactly this — the 1-hour tiered rate is derived by scaling rather than falling back to base, because mixing a tiered rate with a base rate inside one request produced an impossible ordering: the always-more-expensive 1-hour TTL coming out *cheaper* than the 5-minute one. That `1h >= 5m` relationship is structural (2× base input against 1.25×), so it is now asserted directly across every model in the pinned table.

  The premise could not be checked by quotation, and that is worth recording because the previous attempt at this area (#376) guessed and was wrong in the opposite direction. Anthropic no longer publishes a long-context rate table at all: the tier only ever applied to the Sonnet 3.5–4.5 lineage, and those models are now retired from the first-party API. So it was checked by **derivation** from two rules the pricing page does still state — cache multipliers are "relative to base input token rates" (read 0.1×, 5-minute write 1.25×, 1-hour write 2×) and they "stack with other pricing modifiers". Applied to Sonnet 4.5's $6/M above-200K input, that yields $0.60 / $7.50 / $12.00 — digit-for-digit what LiteLLM publishes in its three `*_above_200k_tokens` cache fields. Two independent routes to identical numbers; a single review assertion was not enough, which is why the issue stayed open rather than being patched on sight. Fixes #393.

  Scope, measured rather than assumed: on this machine the change corrects **zero** turns today. Of 102,179 priced turns, none with a >200K prompt is on a tiered-lineage model — all 47,912 long-prompt turns are Opus 4.8 / Opus 5 / Fable 5 / Sonnet 5, every one of which bills flat across the full 1M window.

- **Fast mode is billed as the premium tier it is, instead of at standard rates.** `usage.speed === "fast"` (research preview on Claude Opus 5 and Opus 4.8) costs $10/M input and $50/M output against the standard $5/$25 — double — with the usual cache multipliers on top. The rates are hardcoded because LiteLLM carries `supports_speed` for these models but publishes no fast prices, so there is nothing to parse; the table is recorded next to the pricing-page quote that justifies it. Fast is matched on the raw model id rather than the pricing family key, because Opus 4.6 and 4.7 share that key and bill standard at the provider.

  A turn with no recorded `speed` bills standard. That deliberately inverts the rule the rest of the app follows — that a null `speed` means "unknown", never "standard" — because the alternative is assuming a doubled rate on every transcript written before the field existed. Built against a synthetic fixture: across 1,200 local transcripts and 10,742 assistant turns, `speed` has never once been `fast`, so there is no present-day cost being mis-stated. It is built now because the day one appears, the error is a silent 2× on the most expensive model in the table and nothing in the UI would look wrong.

- **A project's cost no longer drops after the first scan and stays there.** On the file-parse backend, per-file stats are cached in `claude-stats.json` and re-priced from that entry whenever the transcript is unchanged. The entry stored flat token totals only, so every cache hit lost three rate-selecting dimensions at once: the **model** (all tokens attributed to an `unknown` bucket priced as Sonnet, so an Opus-heavy transcript came back far under), the **long-context tier** (everything demoted to base), and the **1-hour cache-write TTL** (everything billed at the 5-minute rate). The first scan of a project was priced correctly and every scan after it was not — which is worse than a consistently wrong number, because it defeats comparison against an earlier screenshot or export. The per-model, per-rate split is now persisted; cache version 1 entries are discarded rather than migrated, since the transcripts are still on disk and one slower scan beats inventing a split that was never recorded. Fixes #394.

- **The session list and the cache-waste diagnostic use the rates that actually apply.** Two consumers were reading rates the cost formula had already moved past, and neither disagreement was visible from one screen. `scanSessionFile` keeps its own token accumulation for the session list's `costEstimate` and was not passing `speed`, so a fast turn priced at half rate on `/sessions` while the aggregate scanner and the SQLite backend priced it correctly. And the cache rebuild-waste figure multiplied by `ModelPricing`'s *base* cache fields, so it ignored the long-context tier, fast mode, and the 1-hour cache-write TTL — the last of which applies to essentially every Claude Code session, understating the waste by ~37% on its own. Rate selection is now a single exported function (`selectEffectiveRates`) that both the bill and the diagnostic go through, so the two cannot drift apart again.

- **Stored per-turn costs re-price themselves after a formula change.** `turns.cost_usd` is stamped at ingest, so the two fixes above would have left the SQLite backend reporting old-formula numbers while the file-parse backend healed via its own cache-version bump — the two disagreeing on exactly the turns the fixes touch. The derivation version is bumped so the corpus re-indexes once on next startup. This costs a full re-parse (~45 min for ~6,000 sessions) that provably changes nothing on this corpus; it is done anyway because a user who did run Sonnet 4/4.5 above 200K would otherwise hit the backend-parity failure class of #220.

- **The test suite no longer depends on a third-party endpoint, or writes a 1.2 MB cache into the working tree.** Model pricing resolves through a disk cache under `resolveStateDir()`, which is `MINDER_STATE_DIR || process.cwd()`. The suite deletes `MINDER_STATE_DIR` (deliberately — it is how DB isolation stays under `os.homedir()`), so under vitest that resolved to the **repo root**: runs read, and on a cache miss *wrote*, `.cache/litellm-pricing.json` next to the source. Wherever that file is absent — every CI runner, and locally the moment its 24 h TTL lapses — pricing came off the network instead, and because `vi.resetModules()` hands each isolated test a fresh module with a fresh single-flight promise, it did so once per reload: **221 requests to `raw.githubusercontent.com` in one measured run**.

  That made a third-party endpoint answering identically 221 times, under 8 parallel forks, an input to the suite. When it didn't, nothing failed loudly — `getModelPricing` returns `pricingMap ?? FALLBACK_PRICING`, so affected forks silently priced from the hardcoded table while others used live rates. A cost stored at *ingest* and the same turn recomputed *live* could therefore be priced from two different tables in one run, which is the shape of the backend-parity divergence in #220.

  Pricing is now pinnable: when **`MINDER_PRICING_FILE`** names a readable JSON file in LiteLLM's schema it is the entire source of truth, with no cache stat, no fetch and no cache write. It is read inside `loadPricing()` rather than at module scope, so it can be isolated per test — a module-scope capture freezes at first import, which is the failure class #331 was about. A named-but-unreadable file falls back to the hardcoded table rather than reaching for the network the setting exists to avoid; that is asserted, because a pin that quietly restores the dependency would be worse than no pin. The suite pins a committed 126-model fixture trimmed to the fields the parser reads (26 KB, not 1.2 MB), so an upstream pricing change can no longer turn this repo red on its own. Verified by running the full suite with all outbound `fetch` rejected and the cache removed: **371 files, 4,824 passing, 0 network attempts**, against 221 attempts and one failure before. Unpinned behaviour is unchanged and still covered.

- **The config-lint panel no longer reports valid agent frontmatter as invalid.** `claude-code-lint` was declared `^0.5.0` and resolved to exactly `0.5.0` — the version published 2026-05-04. This was never a decision: it is the default range `pnpm add` writes, and under semver a caret on a `0.x` version is minor-locked (`^0.5.0` = `>=0.5.0 <0.6.0`), so `0.6.0` and `0.7.x` were structurally unreachable. Dependabot and `pnpm update` were both *correct* to report nothing to do, which is why the drift produced no signal for three months. Upstream 0.6.0 and 0.7.0 are almost entirely false-positive fixes — "stop rejecting valid Claude Code config (6 false positives)", "model the documented `settings.json` surface", "replace invented sandbox/marketplace fields with the documented ones", "model every documented tool, and put the tool list under doc authority", and acceptance of the documented `fable` model — so a frozen linter had been degrading not into silence but into **confident wrongness**, flagging the newest and most correct configs hardest. Measured on this repo before and after the bump: the two `agent-tools` P1 errors on `.claude/agents/frontend-developer.md` and `code-reviewer.md` ("Expected array, received string", against the documented comma-separated `tools:` form) are **gone**, taking the findings the panel actually surfaces from 3 to 1, with **no new findings** — the one upstream change that could have added any, the new `SettingsSchema` key-conformance check, produced none here. Verified beyond the release notes: all nine validator names in `src/lib/lint/library.ts`'s `VALIDATOR_TARGET` map survive in 0.7.1 (a rename would silently drop findings, since unmapped validators are skipped), and every dependency of the packaged CLI still resolves *inside* `dist/minder-server` — including `zod`, the exact version-conflict hazard that made issue #288 subtle, since the app carries zod 3 and the linter needs zod 4. Now `^0.7.1`; note this will freeze at `0.7.x` for the same semver reason, so any pre-1.0 dependency needs a human review cadence rather than trust in automation.

- **A project split across two spellings of its own directory reported only part of its spend.** The encoded Claude project directory preserves whatever case the path was observed with, so on Windows — where the filesystem is case-insensitive — one folder can reach the index as `C--dev-app`, `c--dev-app` and `C--Dev-App` while `toSlug` lowercases all of them to a single slug. The DB-backed usage queries put the raw `project_dir_name` in their `GROUP BY`, turning that into several rows for one project, each holding a fraction of its cost and tokens. Two projects were split this way on the reference index.

  The grouping key now case-folds the directory name, but **only when it has the Windows `X--` drive-prefix shape**. Both halves of that are load-bearing: `toSlug` strips the drive prefix, so `C:\dev\app` and `D:\dev\app` share a slug while being genuinely different projects and must not merge; and POSIX-encoded names come from case-sensitive filesystems, where `/home/me/Dev` and `/home/me/dev` really are two directories. `projectDetails` continues to key on the slug alone, matching the file aggregator, because everything downstream of it — an `IN (...)` fan-out stitched back by slug, and the React key on the breakdown rows — assumes one row per slug.

  The visible symptom was narrower than the defect: two React duplicate-key errors on `/usage`, dev-only. The project filter already deduplicated by slug in the component, so the dropdown looked right while the underlying rows stayed split — worth noting because fixing the keys alone would have silenced the console and left the under-reported cost in place. Fixes #236.
- **PR chips were dropped when a tool result arrived on the top-level `content` field.** `prExtractor` resolved user-turn content with a nullish chain, and `[] ?? x` is `[]` — so when Claude wrote `message.content: []` and put the real blocks on top-level `content`, the `gh pr create` result was never scanned and its PR URL silently lost. Now length-based, matching `ticketExtractor` (fixed there in #185) and `parser.ts`. Recall-only: it never produced a wrong chip, just a missing one. Fixes #186.
- **`/api/usage` could serve a 304 against a stale rolling window.** The ETag was computed from the max file mtime plus the query params, with no time component. Every period except `all` is a rolling window whose totals and daily buckets legitimately change as turns age off the trailing edge — no file is touched, so the mtime never moves and the ETag kept matching. A revalidating client stayed pinned to the old window until some unrelated file changed. The cache slot's own timestamp now rides in the ETag, bounding staleness to the existing 2-minute TTL. Mirrors the same fix on `/api/usage/compare` (#189). Fixes #190.

### Security
- **Env files and `.mcp.json` are no longer copied into the build output.** Next's file tracer sweeps everything under the tracing root that is not explicitly excluded, and neither was on the exclude list — so both were traced into `/api/health`'s and `/api/projects`' manifests and materialized in `.next/standalone`, which is the payload the tray app and the installer are built from. The exclusion covers the whole `.env*` prefix rather than the single `.env.local` that happened to be on disk when this was measured: `.gitignore` covers `.env` and `.env*.local`, and the downstream payload-hygiene gate already rejects any basename starting with `.env`, so the tracer-side boundary now matches the set the other end of the pipeline enforces. They were removed again downstream by `package-standalone.mjs`'s prune, so the shipped installers are not believed to have carried them; the exposure was that a developer's local credentials entered the packaging pipeline at all and were stopped only on the way out. `next.config.ts`'s own comment had already recorded that #284 "shipped a real `.git` and `.env.local` once", which is what that failure looks like when the prune does not catch it. Both are now excluded at the tracer, along with `agentlytics-repo/` — a git-ignored reference checkout inside the tracing root whose 83 files, including a screenshot and a `package-lock.json`, were being traced into every affected route.

## [1.9.1] - 2026-08-08

*One fix, found by checking the browser console while verifying something else. Worth noting how it was mis-filed: the issue diagnosed it as a user-scope-versus-plugin-scope collision, and that was a guess — the duplicate does not exist in user scope at all. Measuring the cache instead of trusting the guess changed both the fix and the estimate of its impact, from a cosmetic React warning to most of the launcher strip's capacity being silently consumed.*

### Fixed
- **Skill launcher chips were mostly duplicates, and the cap was spent on them.** `selectSkillChips` never deduplicated by slug, so every cached copy of a plugin became its own chip: identical label, identical `skill:<slug>` React key, and a console error whose documented consequence is children being duplicated or omitted. The scale is the part worth stating — the catalog indexes each cached plugin version *and* the same plugin mirrored across marketplaces, so on the reference machine **1,122 plugin skill directories reduce to 201 distinct slugs**, with `ai-gateway` present 3 times and `neon` **57**. With `MAX_SKILL_CHIPS = 8` applied after sorting by slug, repeats of a couple of skills consumed the whole strip and every distinct skill below them never rendered.

  Deduplication happens before the cap, so freed slots go to real skills. Where the same slug exists in more than one scope the user-scope copy wins — it is the one the developer installed themselves — and equal-ranked copies keep the first, which is the entire real-world case: cache entries identical but for the version in their path. Which copy survives only affects the tooltip; every chip dispatches the bare `/<slug>`, which resolves the same way regardless. Fixes #405.

## [1.9.0] - 2026-08-08

*A release almost entirely about data Minder already had. **Six of the eight additions surface fields that were already parsed, typed, stored by both backends — and rendered nowhere**: per-session hook runs, Claude Code's own session title, permission-mode switches, subagent provenance, the hook-activity source, and two analytics that existed only as MCP tools. Nothing new was collected for them; the work was noticing they had no surface.*

*The fixes share one shape, and it is worth naming because it recurred five times: **a partial answer shaped exactly like a complete one**. Hook Activity reported 12% of its data as the whole of it. Tool Latency was ~15 days from doing the same. The scatter plot placed 59% of sessions at zero on axes they had never been measured on. A subagent that could not be counted reported `0` turns. A title you could see could not be searched.
