In every case the display was confident and the number was wrong — and in every case the fix was to let the absence show rather than substitute a plausible value.*

*Three PR review rounds on the final change each found that same error in a new disguise, twice in states where wrong data would have rendered as reassurance. Every figure below is measured on the reference index.*

### Fixed
- **The session scatter plot was placing unmeasured sessions on the axis at zero.** `projectScatter` coerced `durationMs`, `oneShotRate` and `maxContextFill` with `?? 0`, so a session that never recorded a measurement was drawn as though it had been measured at the bottom of the scale. On the reference index that is **2,974 of 5,028 sessions (59.1%)** with no peak context fill — a majority of the Context Pressure cloud sitting on the floor, each tooltip stating `0% fill` as a fact. Worse on Reliability, where the same coercion reads as a 0% first-pass success rate: the worst score on the chart, awarded for not having been measured.

  Each preset now plots only the sessions carrying the measurement it needs, and states how many it left out and which measurement they lack — a silently smaller cloud would read as "you have fewer sessions", a different false claim. A session measured *at* zero still plots at zero; the check is `typeof x === "number"`, not truthiness.

  Found by auditing the rest of the recent work for the defect class fixed in #402. Two hazards surfaced while fixing it: the renderer pairs `sessions[i]` with `prepared.x[i]`, so filtering the coordinates without the point list would have made **clicking a dot open a different session** (the filter now returns both together); and the exclusion notice cannot reuse `yLabel`, because Complexity vs Cost excludes on its *x*-axis measurement.

  The existing test asserted the defect — `expect(point.durationMs).toBe(0)` under the heading "safe defaults" — so it read as a deliberate decision and locked the behaviour in. Same failure mode as the `user-invocable` test in #383: a test written from the same premise as the code ratifies it rather than verifying it.

  A preset can now legitimately filter out **every** session — a corpus predating a measurement, or one where OTEL was never enabled — which the old `sessions.length === 0` guard did not cover. That left `Math.min(...[])` / `Math.max(...[])` producing `Infinity` / `-Infinity` scale domains and an unrenderable chart. There is now an explicit empty state naming the missing measurement, rather than claiming there are no sessions.
- **A subagent's turn count is `undefined` where it cannot be counted, no longer `0`.** The SQLite backend does not index sidechain entries, so it has no per-subagent count to give — but it reported `0`, which is indistinguishable from a subagent that genuinely took no turns. Comparing that against Claude Code's own `metaTurnCount` rendered every meta-sourced subagent as an amber "14 turns recorded · 0 counted" disagreement, presenting a documented backend limitation as a data conflict. `SubagentInfo.messageCount` is now optional, and a disagreement is only claimed when two real counts exist and differ.

  **Both** backends manufactured that zero, not just the indexed one. Current Claude Code transcripts carry no sidechain assistant entries at all, so on the file backend the counting loop never runs and the initialiser's `0` stands — the identical defect, one file over. Both now start undefined, so the count materialises only if something was actually counted.
- **The session title is searchable.** Neither title column was: `generated_title` was missed when the title slot was introduced, and `ai_title` joined it above. Searching for the title displayed on a row returned nothing unless the same words happened to appear in a prompt — and neither column is reachable through `prompts_fts`, which indexes turn text, while an `ai-title` entry is session metadata rather than turn text. Both are now in the `titles` retriever and in the client-side filter. Measured on the reference index: of 400 sampled sessions carrying an `ai_title`, **126 are findable only via that column**, and one sample query goes from 9 hits to 17.

- **Hook Activity was reporting 12% of the data as if it were all of it.** The OTEL branch of `getHookActivity` selected `ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 10000` and aggregated in JS, so the newest 10,000 rows survived and everything older was discarded *before* grouping. The tell was that `totalFires` read exactly `10000` for `7d`, `30d` and `all` alike — the cap, not the window, was deciding the answer. Measured on the reference index: 10,000 fires / 104 hook names at every period, against a true 16,552 / 110 for 7d, 65,163 / 172 for 30d, and **81,012 / 193** for all history.

  This is the same defect A6 fixed in the transcript fallback and left in the primary path, where it mattered more — OTEL wins whenever any hook event exists, so it is the branch nearly every machine with telemetry actually renders. Fixed the same way: a `GROUP BY hook_name, duration_ms` histogram, bounded by *distinct* values (46,138 rows) rather than capped by row count, with the counts still describing every fire in the period. A fire whose duration attribute is missing now counts toward `fires` and is excluded from the percentiles, instead of being dropped entirely and quietly under-reporting that hook's frequency.
- **Tool Latency had the same latent truncation, ~15 days from biting.** `getToolLatency` also aggregated in JS behind `ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 50000`, with a comment asserting the accuracy loss was "negligible at that scale" — a claim that had never been measured, and which describes the wrong failure anyway: past the cap the result is not a sample, it is a *recent* population, and `n` is reported to the user as the sample size. It did not truncate yet (34,488 rows all-history) but was growing ~1,020/day, so the `30d` and `all` options added above would have started serving a partial answer within about two weeks of shipping them. Regrouped by `(tool_name, duration_ms, success)`, which compresses 2.61× here and cannot truncate at any corpus size. Durations are re-merged across the success split before the percentiles, so one 20 ms success and one 20 ms failure remain two observations of 20 ms.

### Added
- **Permission Denials and Tool Provenance now have UI.** Both analytics existed only as MCP tools — `get-denial-breakdown` and `get-tool-provenance` had no API route and no component, so the data was reachable only by asking Claude for it. Each now has a route under `/api/telemetry/` and a card in `Stats → Telemetry`, sharing the section's period toggle through the same `resolveSinceParam` the other telemetry routes use.

  **Permission Denials** groups refused calls by kind with the top tools per kind. Its empty state is deliberately not "all clear": `hasData: false` cannot distinguish "nothing was ever refused" from "this index predates the `denial_kind` column", so the card says exactly that. It is also the only card in that section that never needed OTEL — `denial_kind` comes from transcript ingest — which the docs now state, because the section header implies otherwise.

  **Tool Provenance** shows the built-in / MCP / plugin split as stated by `tool_source`, rather than inferred from the `mcp__server__tool` naming convention Minder uses elsewhere — a convention rather than a guarantee, and one that says nothing about plugin-provided tools. Only sources actually observed are listed: a fixed three-way legend would imply plugin tools were measured and found to be zero, which is a different claim from never having seen one. Measured on the reference index: 19,421 built-in / 851 MCP, no plugin events.

  Tool Provenance states its **coverage**, not just its total. The split is computed over calls that state a source, and Claude Code began emitting `tool_source` partway through most indexes — on the reference machine it starts 2026-07-19 while events reach back to 2023-11-14. The card previously showed a bare `20,272 tool events`, so a split describing **57% of all history** (73% at `30d`) looked exactly like one describing every call. It now reads `N of M tool calls state a source (X% of this window)` and says the split covers only those. `getToolProvenance` gained `callsInWindow` and `sourceCoverage`, counted independently of the split so a coverage of 1 means every call stated a source rather than the query agreeing with itself; the percentage is truncated rather than rounded so 99.6% cannot render as `100%` beside a partial-window warning. Numerator and denominator are drawn from the same event population, so coverage cannot exceed 1 — ingestion lifts `tool_source` without checking `event_name`, and a mismatch would have counted an event in the numerator alone, pushing the ratio past 1 where the card renders it as *full* coverage and the fault reads as good news. Caught in review on #406 across three rounds.

  A quiet window is a third state, distinct from both. `hasData` deliberately ignores `since` — it asks whether the column was ever populated, which is what keeps "predates the field" separable from "nothing refused" — while the breakdown honours the period. Collapsing the two rendered `0 denied calls · First-pass success is unknown for every kind here…` for a window that contained no denied turns at all, reachable on the reference index the moment `today` is selected. Now its own state, and the one place this card reports good news plainly: by then the field is known to be populated, so the absence is a real result. Caught in review on #406 by both bots independently.

  The denial × first-pass-success cross is dropped entirely when no kind can supply it, with one footnote explaining why, instead of printing a placeholder on every row. This is the common case and was only visible by running the endpoint: task outcomes are recorded for 1,503 of 175,959 turns (0.85%) and denials are rare, so **zero of the 43 denials overlapped** — four rows of "—" that read as a measurement in progress. A kind that does have a sample still shows its real rate, including a genuine `0%`; the selection logic is extracted to `src/lib/telemetry/denialDisplay.ts` and tested, so a missing numerator returns "unmeasured" rather than the most alarming available reading.
- **One period toggle for the whole Stats → Telemetry section** (`today` / `7d` / `30d` / `all`). The cards previously disagreed by construction: Token Usage and Cache Efficiency each carried their own toggle, while Edit Acceptance, Tool Latency, Hook Activity and Pressure were hard-wired to a 7-day window with no control at all — so the grid could show two different periods at once and never said which was which. The two self-managed cards are now fully controlled by the section, so there is one source of truth and no competing toggles.

  **The cutoff now actually advances, on the clock each period follows.** Hour-bucketing keeps the value stable across renders — without it every render mints a fresh URL and re-fires all six fetches — but stability alone meant a Stats page left mounted across a boundary held its original URLs and data indefinitely, since React does not re-render in response to wall-clock time and nothing on the page polls. A new `useTelemetrySince` schedules the recompute, rescheduled per fire so drift cannot accumulate and a device waking from sleep re-aligns to the real next boundary. All six cards share the one string, so they move together.

  The wake-up is per period, because the cutoffs move on different clocks: `7d`/`30d` on the epoch hour, `today` at **local midnight**, `all` never. Scheduling `today` hourly was wrong beyond the obvious fractional-offset zones (Asia/Kolkata +5:30, Asia/Kathmandu +5:45, America/St_Johns −3:30, where a page open across midnight showed the previous day for up to 45 minutes) — an hourly fire at 22:00 recomputes the same midnight and changes nothing, so the refresh only ever landed when a fire happened to coincide with midnight. `all` arms no timer at all rather than a large one, since `setTimeout` coerces anything past 2³¹−1 to ~1 ms and would spin.

  **One period name now resolves to one cutoff on every path.** The section drives its six cards through two encodings — four take a client-computed `since`, two send the period name for the route to resolve — and `periodToMs` buckets the rolling windows to the hour for *all* callers, including the MCP tools. Bucketing only the client half (as the first cut of this change did) left the two `?period=` cards resolving against a live `Date.now()` server-side, so a single toggle covered windows up to an hour apart and only four of six refetched at an hour boundary: the same "cards disagree" defect, smaller and invisible. Caught in review; a test pins that `periodToMs` and `periodToSince` name the same instant.
- **The Hook Activity card now names its data source.** `HookActivityResult.source` has always distinguished OTEL from transcript, and nothing surfaced it — the two are never blended because a row means a different thing in each (OTEL names the hook, `PreToolUse:Bash`; the transcript names the command it ran, `codegraph sync`), so a fire count is not comparable between them. A badge now says which answered, and the first column header follows it (`Hook` vs `Command`) — labelling transcript rows "Hook" would have announced that the semantics changed while still displaying the old ones. The count of fires nobody timed is also shown as visible text rather than living only in a per-row `title`, which is mouse-only.
- **`get-hook-activity` accepts an explicit `source` (`auto` / `otel` / `transcript`).** `auto` — the previous and still-default behaviour — prefers OTEL and falls back only when it returns nothing, which means the transcript pipeline is unreachable once OTEL has any events: `since` is a *lower* bound, so every window ending at now includes recent events and the fallback never fires at any period. Documentation shipped in this PR claimed the MCP tool was already the workaround for that; it was not, since the tool exposed only `period` and called the identical preference path (caught by Codex review). Naming a source now forces it, and a forced source never falls through — an empty result for the pipeline you asked for is the honest answer, where substituting the other one would return rows keyed on something else entirely. The same control on the Hook Activity card is still open in `TODO.md`.
- **A per-session `Hooks` tab on session detail.** `hookRuns` was fetched, typed, and populated by both storage backends, then dropped on the floor — there was no way to ask what hooks cost one particular session. The tab groups runs by command and ranks them by **total measured time** rather than fire count, which is the ordering that answers "where did this session's wall-clock go". Untimed runs count toward the run count and are excluded from every duration (`12 (9 timed)`, and `—` rather than `0ms`), per the same principle the rest of the hook work follows. Hook failures render below the table with a blocking failure styled distinctly from an advisory one. Presence-gated only, so it works in demo mode and under `MINDER_USE_DB=0`.
- **Claude Code's own session title is now used.** `aiTitle` arrives in the transcript on roughly one session in ten and was read, stored, and rendered nowhere — the detail page offered to *generate* a title while one sat unused in the payload. It now fills the session-row title slot and the detail subtitle, behind Minder's own generated title (which is user-deliberate) and labelled **from Claude Code** so the provenance is not blended.
- **Permission-mode timeline chip.** `permissionModes` was parsed by both storage backends and shown nowhere. Sessions that switched mode now carry a chip with the path taken (`plan → auto`), consecutive repeats collapsed since the entries are a log rather than a path. Omitted entirely when absent — a session that never switched emits no entry, so absence is not `auto`.
- **Subagent cards say where their numbers came from.** `metaSourced` distinguishes a record read from Claude Code's own `agent-*.meta.json` from one inferred off the parent transcript; both were parsed and neither was surfaced, so every card looked equally authoritative. When Claude Code's turn count disagrees with the transcript-derived count the chip turns amber and shows both, rather than silently preferring one — the same reasoning as the Hook Activity source badge and the Stats cross-check card.

## [1.8.0] - 2026-08-06

*Almost all of this release comes from decoding transcript detail Claude Code has been writing since ~2.1.212 that Minder was reading none of, plus the analytics that detail unlocks. A theme worth stating because it shaped the work: **six of the eleven planned slices had a factually wrong premise**, each caught by measuring the corpus rather than trusting the plan — a field that does not exist, a directory that does not exist, a scraper that could not be retired, a table that had never received a row, a subagent tree that is not reconstructible, and a three-way split that is not in the data. Every figure quoted below is measured on the reference index, not extrapolated.*

### Added
- **`get-hook-activity` now works without OTEL, and says which pipeline answered.** It read only `hook_execution_complete` events, so for anyone who hadn't enabled OTEL telemetry — the default — it returned an empty result that reads as "you have no hooks" rather than "I can't see them". It now falls back to the transcript-derived hook runs, which need no setup and cover all history retroactively. The two are never blended: OTEL names a hook (`PreToolUse`) while the transcript names the command it ran (`codegraph sync`), so merging them would count one execution twice under two labels. A `source` field says which was used — added specifically because an unexplained empty result is the failure mode that hid the bug above.
- **Permission denials, grouped by why the call was refused and crossed with first-pass success.** New `get-denial-breakdown` MCP tool over `tool_uses.denial_kind`: `permission-rule`, `automode-blocked`, `automode-unavailable`, `user-rejected`, each with the tools most often refused that way. The cross with `turns.task_outcome` answers whether a denial actually derails the work or the model routes around it — a kind with many denials and an unchanged first-pass rate is friction; one that tanks the rate is a rule worth revisiting. This is the A6 half of the reuse A2 built `task_outcome` for, and it is a JOIN rather than a second rollup precisely because A2 made it a general turn-level join key.

  **`user-rejected` is counted separately from rule denials**, because they mean opposite things — configuration you can change versus you disagreeing with the model — and Claude Code 2.1.216 had to fix its own telemetry for miscounting one as the other. A turn with several calls denied the same way counts its task **once**: without that, numerator and denominator inflate together, so the rate still looks right while the sample size is fiction. A kind with no denials reports *no data* rather than zero, since "nothing was ever refused" and "this index predates the field" are not the same claim.

- **OTEL telemetry can now be joined to the transcript turns it describes, and tool provenance is read rather than inferred.** Two datasets that overlap almost entirely had no key between them. New `get-tool-provenance` MCP tool reports tool events by `tool_source` — `builtin` / `mcp` / `plugin`, stated by Claude Code instead of inferred from the `mcp__server__tool` naming convention, which is a convention rather than a guarantee and says nothing about plugin-provided tools. Locally: 18,605 builtin, 851 mcp.

  **The plan named a key that does not exist.** C3 was specified against `message.uuid`, said to have been added to OTel log events in 2.1.214. An enumeration of every attribute key across 4,000 tool and API events found `user.account_uuid` and `request_id` — nothing else uuid-shaped, under any spelling. The key that works is `request_id`, and it was already on both sides all along: `requestId` on assistant transcript entries, `attrs.request_id` on `api_request` events. Verified by intersecting the full corpus rather than assumed: **71,466 of 205,137** assistant turns (34.8%) match an OTEL event, with all 39,139 `api_request` events carrying a distinct value.

  **Coverage is reported, never assumed.** That 34.8% is the honest answer, not a defect — telemetry is opt-in and retained for a window, so most historical turns have no event and never will. A correlation that silently dropped the unmatched ones would present a third of the data as the whole of it, so the ratio ships alongside the figures. The count uses `EXISTS` rather than a join, because one turn produces several events (`api_request`, `tool_result`, …) and a plain join would count it once per event and report coverage above 100%.

  Both values are lifted from `payload_json` into indexed columns (schema v24) and **backfilled**. `JSON_EXTRACT` cannot use an index, so correlating by request id meant a full scan of a 641k-row table per lookup — measured while writing this, 300 lookups did not finish in ten minutes. The backfill matters because OTEL events are append-only telemetry that nothing ever re-ingests: without it the columns would only ever describe events recorded after the upgrade, and all existing history would look like it had no request ids. `DERIVED_VERSION` 16 populates the transcript side; it stacks on A6's un-run v15 re-parse, so both collapse into one pass.

  Also checked: the plan asked whether Minder's Prometheus path was compensating for the invalid `# UNIT` lines Claude Code fixed in 2.1.216. There is no Prometheus exporter in `src/` — the word appears only in the plan document — so there was nothing to compensate and nothing to undo.
- **Sessions that hit a delegation cap are flagged, because Claude Code truncates them silently.** 2.1.212/217 added hard per-session limits — 200 subagent spawns, 200 web searches, 20 concurrent subagents, spawn depth 3 — and a session that hits one stops rather than finishing, with nothing in the transcript saying why. It reads exactly like a session that chose to finish. Sessions at or near a cap now carry a warning chip; sessions comfortably inside get nothing, because a badge on every session that used a handful of subagents is noise, and noise is how a real one gets missed.

  The chip says a cap was **reached**, never that anything was *blocked*. Two of the caps are configurable, so Minder can see the count but not the ceiling actually in force on that machine — claiming a block would be asserting something it cannot know.

  **Concurrency and depth are reported as unmeasured, not as zero.** The plan assumed subagent trees were already reconstructible; they are not on this data. Concurrency is an instantaneous property a finished transcript cannot recover, and depth needs parent-to-child linkage — `turns.parent_tool_use_id` exists for exactly that and is NULL on every row of the reference index. Rendering them as `0` would show a session nested five deep as comfortably inside a cap of three.

  The planned notification-rule integration is **not** included, and the reason is structural rather than effort: rules match against individual hook payloads, so "this session has spawned 200 subagents" is not expressible in them — it needs a running count the rule engine does not carry. Wiring it would have meant either a rule that fires on every spawn or one that never fires.
- **New `/workflows` page cataloging the multi-agent orchestration scripts Claude Code runs.** One row per workflow with its description, when-to-use, declared phases, run count, last run, and the projects it has run in. Gated by a new default-on `workflowCatalog` feature flag. Namespaced `ClaudeWorkflowEntry` throughout, because a project's CI/CD panel already uses "workflows" for GitHub Actions and the two are unrelated.

  **The plan had the location wrong, and the difference reshaped the feature.** C1 specified `.claude/workflows/`, mirroring the skills and commands walkers. No such directory exists — not at user level, not in any project. The Workflow tool persists per *session*: `~/.claude/projects/<project>/<session-id>/workflows/scripts/<name>-<runId>.js`, alongside a `wf_<runId>.json` run record. A session-scoped layout means the same workflow appears once per run — 30 files on this machine resolving to **20 distinct workflows** — so the catalog folds runs into one entry rather than listing a directory. That fold is also what makes it useful: "the code-review workflow ran 5 times" is a question a per-directory walker could never answer.

  **Scripts are parsed statically, never executed.** A workflow script is arbitrary JavaScript that spawns subagents; running one to read its name would be absurd. The `meta` object is a pure literal by contract, which is exactly what makes a conservative static read safe. It is JavaScript rather than frontmatter or JSON — bare identifier keys, mixed quote styles, trailing commas — so it cannot delegate to `JSON.parse`, and pulling in a full JS parser for one object literal was not worth the dependency. Validated against all 30 real scripts: **30 of 30 parse with zero warnings**, phases included.

  Two parser subtleties, both caught by tests rather than by reading: brace counting has to ignore braces inside strings and comments, or a description containing `{this}` truncates the object; and the key scan has to run *before* the string-opening branch, because a JSON-style key is itself quoted (`{"title":…}`) and would otherwise be consumed as a value — which silently dropped every `phases` array, since that is exactly how the Workflow tool serialises them. A workflow whose `meta` cannot be read is still listed under its filename, with a lint chip: one that exists is more useful shown than hidden behind a parse error.
- **Tooltip-only explanations are now reachable without a mouse.** `title` is a mouse-only affordance: it does not appear on keyboard focus in any major browser, touch devices have no hover, and screen-reader support is inconsistent. Where a tooltip was the *only* place a piece of information existed, that information was unreachable for keyboard, touch and screen-reader users. Fixed for the load-bearing subset — chips whose tooltip defines a jargon term or supplies a missing unit — using the repo's existing `.sr-only` + `aria-hidden` pattern. Per issue #380 this is deliberately not a sweep of all 269 `title=` usages; most restate visible text and are harmless.

  The biggest win is `QualityChip`, fixed once in the shared component: `compaction loop`, `tool fail streak` and `resume anomaly` are jargon whose entire meaning lived in the tooltip, and every call site inherits the fix. The most consequential is the git **`status unavailable`** indicator — it sits exactly where `N uncommitted` would, so a reader who cannot reach the tooltip sees no dirty count and concludes the repo is clean. There, the failure state was indistinguishable from success. Also covered: the effort-mix chip (why the mix doesn't sum to the turn count), the project `live?` badge (what exactly is uncertain), and the skills catalog's two adjacent unitless numbers — one dollars of attributed spend, one a dispatch count, which is the distinction that catalog exists to draw.

  **The git-status fix initially went into a component nothing renders.** `GitStatus` and `GitStatusCompact` live in one file; `ProjectCard` and `ProjectDetail` both import the compact one, and nothing imports the other. The first pass fixed only `GitStatus`, so in every shipped flow a screen-reader user still met a bare `?` — worse than the full variant's `status unavailable`, since a question mark carries no meaning at all without its tooltip. Both variants now share one explanation constant, and the test asserts the caveat is present in whichever variant the consuming components actually render, rather than in whatever the file happens to export first.

  **This reaches screen readers and not the other two audiences.** `.sr-only` is visually clipped, so a *sighted* keyboard or touch user still cannot see these explanations — and issue #380 names all three. The issue prescribed exactly this pattern, so following it faithfully still leaves the hover-less case unsolved; closing that needs a tooltip primitive that opens on focus and tap, which is a shared-UI change rather than a per-chip one and is tracked in #391. Recorded here because "improved accessibility" would otherwise read as "fixed", and the git-status case in particular still fails silently for a touch user.

  Two things the audit turned up. The issue lists `EffortMixChip` as already fixed by #379 — it was not; that fix landed on the effort *panel*. And a naive `title=` scan over-reports badly, because React components take `title` **props** that render visibly (`MemoryTriage`'s section headings, for one) — those look identical to an HTML tooltip in a grep and need no fix at all.
- **PR chips now say where the link came from.** Minder learns about a session's pull requests two ways: a `pr-link` entry Claude Code writes itself (**recorded** — URL, number and repository are reported), or a URL matched by regex in `gh pr create` output (**scraped** — everything inferred, including the repo, which is recovered from the URL). Until now the two were indistinguishable once merged. Each link now carries its source through `session_prs` (schema v22) and both read backends, and the chip's tooltip says which produced it. It matters when a link looks wrong: a scraped link can be a false positive in a way a recorded one cannot — `gh pr create` answering "a pull request for branch X already exists: <url>" reads exactly like a successful create.

  **The scraper was expected to be retired here, and the measurement says keep it.** A1 anticipated that once `pr-link` entries covered the corpus the regex could go. Across 5,319 local transcripts (17,015 `pr-link` entries), recorded entries find **738** distinct PR URLs to the scraper's **657** — 86 the regex never sees, which is the win this slice was after. But **5 URLs are found only by the scraper**, and every one came from a session that *did* emit `pr-link` entries for its other PRs. So the gap is not "these transcripts predate the feature", which would age out by itself; it is the CLI's own recording missing cases. Deleting the scraper would have lost real links immediately and kept losing them.

  Existing rows are deliberately **not** backfilled. NULL means "indexed before the column existed", not "scraped" — most of those 652 overlapping rows are in fact recorded, and guessing would write a wrong provenance that a re-parse then has to undo. `DERIVED_VERSION` 15 drives the re-parse that supplies the real answer. That bump is load-bearing, not housekeeping: neither path a session can take would have filled the column on its own, since an unchanged transcript hits the no-op gate and never gets re-read, while a growing one appends from the tail and never revisits PR entries already indexed in the prefix. Without it every pre-upgrade chip would have kept unknown provenance permanently.

- **Spend is now attributed to the skill and MCP server that caused it, not the one that happened to be called.** `/usage` and each project's Costs tab gain a **What caused the spend** panel driven by Claude Code's own per-turn attribution rather than by inference over tool-call names. The two answer different questions and the gap is not marginal — measured on the reference index, attribution accounts for **11x** more MCP spend ($2,051.96 vs $186.62) and **373x** more skill spend ($2,441.87 vs $6.54, the latter across just 8 turns).

  **The plan predicted the opposite direction and was wrong.** It assumed call-site inference would *over*-attribute; it massively *under*-attributes, for a structural reason. The turn that issues a tool call is tiny — often a lone `tool_use` block — while the expensive turn is the **next** one, which pulls a large tool result into context and reasons over it. Inference finds the cheap turn and misses the costly one it caused. Skills show it most starkly, because `tool_uses.skill_name` only fires on an explicit `Skill` invocation: a skill like `pr-resolve` that then drives 6,528 turns costing $1,449 is invisible to it. **The gap is not a naming artifact** — restricting the comparison to the 13 servers present under both signals still gives **10.6x**.

  Both signals are kept, per their different meanings: call *counts* continue to use inference (`mcpParser.ts` is untouched), and only *cost* uses attribution. A list is wholly explicit or wholly inferred and carries a `method` saying which — never mixed, since blending series that differ by an order of magnitude is worse than showing either alone. Periods with no explicit attribution fall back to inference and are badged **estimated**, with the tooltip stating the figure is a floor rather than a total.

  Server identity needed folding to make this work at all. The explicit field carries the real id (`plugin:playwright:playwright`, `claude.ai Vercel`) while the inferred name is recovered from an already-encoded *tool* name (`mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__…`), so the same server arrives under two spellings — and both occur within 35 sessions on this corpus. `mcpServerKey` folds every non-alphanumeric character to `_`, matching the tool-name encoding; validated at 13 of 18 names matching with **zero collisions**. The mapping is deliberately one-way: `claude_ai_Vercel` could restore to `claude.ai Vercel` or `claude:ai:Vercel` and nothing distinguishes them.

  Each server row names its costliest **tools**. A server total says "Playwright cost $634"; it does not say what to do about it — `browser_take_screenshot` returning an image costs far more per call than `browser_click` returning nothing, so the split turns a number into a decision about which call to stop making. Explicit path only: `attribution_mcp_tool` has no call-site counterpart worth trusting at that granularity, so an estimated list omits it rather than fabricating one. The SQL groups by `(server, tool)` in the same scan as the server totals, so the two cannot drift apart under a filter change.

  The **skills catalog** now shows attributed spend beside the invocation count, with a "Most expensive" sort. The two disagree sharply and that is the point: a skill invoked once that then drives hours of work is expensive, and the count alone says the opposite. A skill with attributed spend but no recorded invocation gets a row — a normal combination, not a glitch. **The agents catalog is deliberately unchanged**: `AgentStats` already carries cost from its own subagent sessions, which is a more direct measure than attribution, and `attribution_skill` does not apply to agents.

  Each skill also reports **first-pass success**, reusing `turns.task_outcome` from the effort work rather than growing a rollup of its own — that column was made a general turn-level join key for exactly this. It answers something counts cannot: `pr-resolve` drove $1,449 across 156 verified tasks at 87% first-pass, `simplify` $483 at 83%. A2's `MIN_TASKS_FOR_RATE` threshold and the shared `n=` sample pill are reused rather than reinvented, which matters here because most skills sit far below it — `improve` shows 100% off two tasks. Skills and servers below 1% of attributed spend fold into one visible "N more" row rather than being dropped.

  Two scoping rules keep the estimated path honest, both found in review. **Repeated calls count once per turn, not once per call**: the estimate is derived by joining tool calls to the turns that issued them, and a turn making several calls to one server — routine, since parallel tool calls are common — appeared once per call, charging its cost as many times over. The turn *count* stayed correct throughout, which is precisely why the inflated total read as trustworthy. **Subagent turns are excluded from estimates but included in attribution**, which sounds inconsistent and is not: attribution is recorded per turn and subagent turns carry it, so a delegating skill rightly keeps its delegate's spend, whereas estimates are derived from tool calls that are deliberately not indexed for subagents. Counting them on one side only would have made the same figure differ by backend. For the same reason, a subagent dispatching a skill no longer inflates that skill's invocation count.
- **Spend and volume split by how each session was started.** `/usage` and each project's Costs tab gain a **By Entrypoint** panel separating interactive terminal sessions from SDK-driven ones (`cli` / `sdk-cli` / `sdk-py`), because pooling them makes every per-session average meaningless. Measured on the reference index, SDK-driven runs are **57.5% of sessions but 3.4% of spend**, while interactive sessions are 41% of sessions and **95.8% of spend** — $12.21 per session against $0.28, a ~44x difference. An unsegmented "average cost per session" blends those into a figure that describes neither. Each row shows its share of *sessions* and its share of *spend* side by side — they rarely match, since automated runs are numerous and short while interactive ones are few and long, and either number alone supports a confident wrong conclusion about where the money goes. Average cost per session is shown too, as the figure that compares like with like.

  **The planned design did not survive contact with the data.** This slice was specified against `sessionKind`, said to carry an `interactive` / `attached` / `unattended` split. A full-corpus scan of all 3,685 transcripts found `sessionKind` on **5 sessions (0.1%)**, only ever valued `bg` — it is a marker for background runs, not an enum, and the three-way split does not exist. `entrypoint` is the axis with real discriminating power, present on all but 50 of 4,895 indexed sessions. `sessionKind: bg` is kept as a flag *on top of* the entrypoint rather than a peer row: all five observed `bg` sessions were `cli`, so promoting it to its own bucket would have moved backgrounded interactive runs out of the Interactive row and quietly changed what that row counts. Two cautions are recorded in `entrypoint.ts`. `sdk-py` appeared in **none** of a 400-file recent sample and surfaced only on the full-corpus pass, so the vocabulary must be enumerated over the whole history. And a file walk misses the ~1,256 subagent transcripts the indexer ingests — which are overwhelmingly `cli`, since a subagent inherits its parent's entrypoint — so measured that way the split reads as 88% SDK-driven, nearly the opposite of what the panel shows. Every figure quoted here is from the index.

  This is the first **session-scoped** breakdown on the usage report — every other one rolls up turns — so `sessions` is a distinct-session count on both backends and the average divides by it. Sessions are attributed to the period they *spent* in, matching the cost sums, which means the column need not add up to the report's total session count; that counts every session with any turn at all. Both backends are covered and pinned by a parity test, including that the file-parse reader decodes `entrypoint` from `attachment` entries rather than assistant turns — the same carrier surprise that reshaped A1.
- **Cost and first-pass success rate, broken down by reasoning effort.** `/usage` and each project's Costs tab gain a **By Effort** panel showing what `low`/`medium`/`high`/`xhigh` each cost alongside how often work started at that level passed verification first time. The pairing is the feature: cost alone makes `xhigh` look like waste, and a success rate alone ignores what the success cost. Together they answer whether raising effort buys a better outcome or just a larger bill. Sessions that recorded effort also show a mix chip (`high×12 · xhigh×3`) in the browser and an **Effort** cell on the detail page.

  Getting the one-shot half required a schema change rather than a query. "One-shot" is a property of a *sequence* — edit → verify → result — so it cannot be recovered by grouping turns, and until now it existed only as two session-level totals that could be crossed with nothing finer than a whole session. Schema v21 adds `turns.task_outcome`, which records each task's verdict against the turn that **started** it (the edit — the work the verification judges, not the turn that ran the verification). That makes first-pass success joinable to any turn-level dimension, so the later attribution and denial analytics reuse the same column instead of each growing a rollup of its own.

  Non-Claude adapter sessions (Codex/Gemini) are covered on both backends: their ingest path builds turns through a separate constructor that derived session-level one-shot counts but stamped no per-turn outcome, so their Edit → test cycles counted toward the headline rate while being invisible to the effort cross-tab — and only on the SQLite backend, since the file backend re-walks the turns live.

  **A rate is suppressed below 30 verified tasks**, showing `—` plus the shared amber `n=` sample pill the Stats page already uses for low-confidence OTEL metrics, rather than inventing a second visual language for the same idea. Every row carries its `n=` count, suppressed or not, so a rate's denominator is always visible. The first full re-index made the reason concrete: `high` anchored 497 tasks but `xhigh` anchored 20 and `medium` 47, and at n=20 a 70% rate carries a 95% interval of roughly 48–86% — overlapping `high` entirely while the chart drew them as a confident 14-point gap. The threshold is a display rule only; `/api/usage`, the MCP tools and the CSV export always report the true ratio. A caveat under the panel notes that higher effort is usually chosen for harder work, so the rates describe what happened at each setting rather than what the setting caused — without it the table reads as "more effort, worse outcomes," which the confounding makes the least likely explanation.

  `unknown` is a real bucket, never folded into `medium`: it covers pre-2.1.212 transcripts, the small share of recent turns Claude Code omits the field on, and sessions not yet re-indexed. On a long history it is usually the largest bucket, and when it is the *only* one the panel says so rather than drawing a single meaningless bar. A level that started no verified tasks shows `—`, not `0%` — the two are opposite readings, and `0%` would also sort it below a level that genuinely failed everything.

  `DERIVED_VERSION` 14 drives the re-parse that computes the new column. This is a second bump on top of A1's, which reserved itself for the whole wave; the justification is that `task_outcome` is not in the JSONL under any name — it is detector output over a full session, so no migration can backfill it and no read-side query can recover it. At the time of writing the v13 re-parse had not yet run, so both bumps collapse into one re-parse.
- **Minder now decodes the transcript detail Claude Code has been writing since ~2.1.212 and it was reading none of.** Nine entry types went unhandled by both JSONL readers, and a set of assistant-turn fields were dropped on the floor: reasoning `effort` (present on 10,288 of 10,742 assistant turns in a 1,200-file sample — `high`/`medium`/`xhigh`), `message.usage.speed` (fast mode, which bills at roughly double), causal skill/MCP attribution, per-session kind and entrypoint, Claude Code's own generated session title, the permission-mode timeline, hook runs with their durations, and authoritative PR links. Schema v20 adds `turns.{effort, attribution_skill, attribution_mcp_server, attribution_mcp_tool}`, `sessions.{session_kind, ai_title, entrypoint}`, `tool_uses.denial_kind`, and the one-to-many `session_hook_runs` / `session_permission_modes` tables; `DERIVED_VERSION` 13 drives the one re-parse that backfills them across existing history. This is plumbing with no UI of its own — it is the decode layer the effort, attribution, session-kind and hook analytics build on, and it ships alone so the re-parse is paid once.

  Two findings changed the shape of the work versus what was planned. `sessionKind` and `entrypoint` turned out to ride **`attachment`** entries rather than assistant turns, so a reader looking at assistant entries would have found nothing and reported a plausible `undefined` forever. And `ai-title` / `permission-mode` entries carry **no `timestamp` at all**, while both readers skip untimestamped entries — decoding them below that guard would have silently dropped every one without erroring. Both are pinned by tests.

  **Causal attribution is deliberately kept separate from the existing `tool_uses.skill_name` / `mcp_server`.** Those are *inferred* from the `mcp__server__tool` naming convention and answer "was this call a skill invocation?" — a different question from "which skill is responsible for this turn's tokens existing?". Conflating them attributes every turn after a tool result to that server instead of only the turns that consumed it, which is the bug this exists to avoid. Both are kept: the inferred pair for call counts, the new columns for cost.

  Three things surfaced in review and are fixed here rather than deferred. **PR links recorded by Claude Code now reach the default SQLite backend**, not just the file-scan reader — the decode moved into the shared `extractPrsFromEntries`, since that is what the DB path persists through; decoding it in the scanner alone meant `session_prs` stayed empty for any PR opened via the web UI, `gh pr create --web`, or a script. **Session metadata arriving in a tail append is no longer lost**: a tail window containing only an `ai-title` or `permission-mode` entry produces no turns, and the parser's "nothing to persist" guard returned early while still advancing the file cursor — so a live session's later title and mode switches were dropped permanently rather than deferred. The tail path now also writes those columns and appends (never replaces) hook runs and mode changes. **`tool_uses.denial_kind` is actually populated**: `toolDenialKind` sits on the *user* entry reporting the tool result while the tool-call row belongs to the preceding *assistant* turn, so it is paired by `tool_use_id` like the existing error map — without that the column would have existed and stayed permanently NULL.

  **Every new field is optional, and absence is never rendered as a default.** A turn with no `effort` is not a `medium` turn; a session with no recorded permission-mode changes is not one that stayed in the default mode. Empty collections collapse to `undefined` rather than to `[]`, so "not recorded" and "recorded as zero" stay distinguishable — which matters because "not yet re-indexed" and "this transcript predates the field" are indistinguishable by design, and both must land in an explicit unknown bucket.

### Fixed
- **Hook latency has never been recorded — the table shipped empty and stayed empty on every machine.** `session_hook_runs` arrived with A1 (schema v20) and held **0 rows** on a fully-reconciled 1.5 GB index. Both readers looked for `hookInfos` on assistant entries; it rides `type:"system"` entries — **4,189 of 4,189 carriers** across the local corpus, and zero assistant ones. In the SQLite path the decode sat below `if (entry.type === "system") { … continue; }`, so the guard reached it first; in the file-parse path it sat *inside* the assistant branch. Neither could ever fire.

  Nothing errored, and because both readers were wrong the same way, the dual-backend parity that has caught every other A-wave divergence held perfectly — at zero. An empty latency table reads exactly like "no hooks configured", which is how it survived the slice that shipped it.

  What was invisible, now measured across the same corpus: **20,284 hook executions**, led by `codegraph sync` at **4,174 ms average × 4,189 runs — 4.9 hours** of wall-clock, and a stop-gate hook at 1,745 ms × 4,189. The plan's illustrative guess for that first hook was 1,450 ms; it is 2.9× worse than the example used to justify building this.

  A hook recorded **without** a duration counts as a fire but contributes no percentile — that is "not measured", not "instant", and rendering it as 0 ms would rank the unmeasured hooks as the fastest on the machine. Hook *failures* (185 non-empty on this corpus) land in a new `session_hook_errors` table (schema v23) rather than as a column on the run: they arrive as a sibling list of plain strings, so a failure genuinely cannot be attributed to a specific command, and inventing that attribution would be worse than not having it.

  `DERIVED_VERSION` 15 drives the re-parse. Unavoidable: the data exists only in the JSONL, so without it every unchanged session keeps its v14 stamp, skips re-parse, and its hook history stays permanently invisible.

- **Minder rejected 22 of Claude Code's 31 hook events, and misread half the boolean spellings skills are allowed to use.** Two small parity gaps against recent Claude Code releases, both of which fail by making something invisible rather than by erroring.

  **Hook events.** `POST /api/hooks` validated against a hand-written list of 9 events, so a hook wired to `PostCompact`, `SubagentStart`, `PermissionDenied`, `FileChanged`, `DirectoryAdded` — or any of the other 17 documented events — sent its payload and got a 400. The list existed three times over: as a type union, as a `Set` in the ingest route, and as a `Set` in the notification-rule validator. All three now derive from one constant, which closes a drift whose failure mode was quiet in a nastier way: a rule targeting an event the validator accepted but the route rejected could be saved and could never fire. Accepting an event is separate from modelling its payload — `DirectoryAdded` gets typed `directory`/`source` fields, and everything newly accepted is captured with its event name, timing and raw body rather than dropped. Raw, deliberately: inventing field names for a schema nobody has read produces a column that is permanently `undefined` and reads as a bug in the producer.

  **Skill frontmatter.** Claude Code accepts `yes` / `no` / `on` / `off` / `1` / `0` case-insensitively wherever it accepts `true` / `false` (2.1.218). Minder compared against `true` and the string `"true"` only, so a skill declaring `user-invocable: yes` was invocable in Claude Code and read as **not** invocable here — dropping it from the workflow launcher chips and from every filter keyed on the flag. The trap is that YAML looks like it should handle this: js-yaml runs the YAML 1.2 core schema, where `yes`/`on` arrive as plain strings and `1`/`0` arrive as *numbers*, while YAML 1.1 — and most people's memory of it — treats all of them as booleans. The catalog also now decodes `disable-model-invocation`, `background`, `context`, `effort` and `model`, and surfaces the first as a `manual only` chip: it narrows *who* can invoke a skill rather than switching it off, which is exactly the kind of thing the browser exists to show. An undeclared key stays `undefined` rather than defaulting to false, so "didn't say" and "said no" remain distinguishable. A skill that is *also* not user-invocable reads `not invocable` instead, since with both doors shut "type it as a slash command" is advice for a command that does nothing.

  **Two events that resolve a turn were being read as if they did not.** `PermissionDenied` conclusively ends a permission prompt, but was absent from the allowlist of events that clear the awaiting state, so a denied prompt kept its project pinned in the awaiting UI until the live session was evicted five minutes later — the events that would clear it only arrive if you go on to do something else. And Claude Code emits `PostToolUseFailure` *instead of* `PostToolUse` when a call fails, so a payload need not repeat `is_error`/`return_code`; reading only the payload left the failure flag unset on precisely the events that are failures by name, and the failure scan walked past them to an older successful call. The Agent View failure badge and any notification rule keyed on `tool.failed` missed exactly the failures they exist to catch.

  **The `user-invocable` default was inverted, and that was the expensive half.** Claude Code's rule is that both you and Claude can invoke any skill unless the frontmatter says otherwise — `user-invocable: false` is what *hides* one from the `/` menu. Minder read a missing key as `false`. Since `selectSkillChips` filters on `userInvocable === true`, the great majority of skills — which never mention the key — were withheld from the workflow-launcher chips and reported in the catalog as not user-invocable while Claude Code would happily run them as a slash command. A test asserted the wrong behaviour too, which is why the boolean-spelling work above passed straight over it: a test agreeing with the implementation is not evidence the implementation is right when both were written from the same assumption. Only an explicit `user-invocable: false` now removes a skill.

  **Distinct skills could share one id.** The id is derived from plugin name, layout and directory basename, so a manifest declaring two roots that each hold a `helper/SKILL.md` produced one id for two different skills. They survive the path-based dedupe — different files — and then collide in every id-keyed consumer, so the detail page could open a different skill than the row that was clicked. Only colliding ids are qualified (by the plugin-relative directory, which is stable under unrelated additions); ids appear in URLs, so rewriting all of them to fix a rare collision would be the more disruptive repair.

  **Plugin skills paths.** A plugin manifest may point `skills` somewhere other than `skills/`, including `"."` for a repo that *is* one skill (2.1.218). Minder only ever opened `<plugin>/skills`, so those plugins contributed nothing to the catalog. Declared paths are now resolved and walked, rejecting any that escape the plugin directory, with a root-level `SKILL.md` recognised as the single skill it is rather than being missed for having no child directory to descend into.

  Also fixed while in there: `walkSkillsRoot` wrapped entry *construction* in the same `try` as the file read, so an error building an entry was indistinguishable from "this directory has no `SKILL.md`" and the walker returned a short but plausible-looking catalog. Only the read is guarded now. This cost real debugging time during this change — an empty list that looks like a legitimate answer is far harder to notice than an exception.

  And a third duplicated definition, of the same family as the hook-event lists: the client-side `SkillRow.entry` was a hand-maintained structural copy of `SkillEntry`, so a field added to the walker reached the browser at runtime while the client's type denied it existed. It now *is* `SkillEntry` — there was never a client/server boundary reason for the copy, since `@/lib/indexer/types` is a pure type module with no imports and the same file already pulled `Provenance` from it. Worth noting how it surfaced: the copy was internally consistent, so the error appeared only where a component read the new field, and only under the production build's type pass.
- **Offline pricing never applied the long-context tier — but the tier applies to far fewer models than it looked.** `FALLBACK_PRICING` published no above-200k rates on any entry, so whenever pricing resolved offline (LiteLLM unreachable with a cold disk cache, or any `getModelPricing` call landing before `loadPricing()` resolves) a >200K prompt was billed entirely at base rates. Reported as issue #376, which was right about the symptom and wrong about the remedy: it proposed adding tiered rates to every entry with a 1M context window. Anthropic's pricing page says the opposite — *"Claude 4.6 and later models … include the full 1M token context window at standard pricing"* — so Opus 5 / 4.8 / 4.7 / 4.6, Sonnet 5 / 4.6 and Fable 5 are flat however long the prompt runs, and surcharging them would have converted a safe under-report into an over-report on precisely the models in daily use. **A 1M context window does not imply a long-context surcharge.** LiteLLM's own table agrees: `claude-sonnet-4-5` publishes above-200k rates, `claude-sonnet-4-6` and `claude-sonnet-5` do not.

  The tier is real only for the Sonnet 3.5–4.5 lineage, whose 1M window shipped as a priced beta at 2× input / 1.5× output. Since Minder's fallback deliberately collapsed *every* Sonnet generation onto one entry — they share base rates — that entry could not express the tier without applying it to Sonnet 5 and 4.6 as well. It is now split on the same 4.6 boundary the pricing page draws: two entries with identical base rates that differ **only** in whether the tier exists. The unknown-Claude-id and empty-string sentinels resolve to the flat entry, not the tiered one, because that sentinel prices an *aggregate* bucket — giving it a tiered model would let a summed input trip the tier and overcharge every ordinary turn in it, re-arming the exact regression the `base`/`long` bucket split was added to prevent.

  Publishing tier rates offline for the first time also made a latent overlay bug reachable. A user pricing rule cannot express above-200k rates; #375 fixed them being silently **dropped** by an override, but preserving them unchanged is wrong for a different reason — a rule setting $10/MTok against a $3 base left the above-200k rate at the provider's $6, so the override applied to short prompts only and the "discounted" long rate ended up *below* the discounted base. Input and output overrides now scale the matching tier rate by the same ratio, exactly as the 1-hour cache-write rate already did.

  The end-to-end coverage gap the issue also flagged is closed: with a fallback model finally publishing a tier, a test can drive `scanClaudeConversations` and assert that many sub-200K turns summing past the boundary stay at base rates, with the mirror case (one genuinely long turn) proving the tier still fires. Separately checked and deliberately **not** changed: fast mode is a genuine premium rate ($10/$50 for Opus 5 / 4.8) that Minder does not price, but a scan of all 5,319 local transcripts found `speed: "fast"` on **0 of 164,252** assistant turns, so wiring it would be speculative rather than corrective.
- **The skills catalog's DB path returned nothing whenever no `Skill` tool had been dispatched.** `loadSkillUsageFromDb` bailed out early on an empty invocation query — harmless while invocations were the only signal, and load-bearing the moment attributed spend was added: the early return skipped the attribution query entirely, handed the façade an empty list, and let it fall back to file-parse, so the DB backend reported no attributed skills at all. The two queries have genuinely different populations (a skill can drive thousands of turns without inference recording a single invocation), so the early return is gone; the empty-result contract the façade relies on is preserved, since it still returns `[]` when neither query finds anything. Caught by a test asserting *which backend served the request* rather than only that the answer looked right — the fallback produced a correct result by the wrong route, which is exactly how a dead query survives.
- **The file-parse backend was missing every subagent transcript — roughly a quarter of the corpus.** Newer Claude Code writes subagent turns to `<session>/subagents/*.jsonl` instead of inlining them into the parent file. The SQLite reconciler walks one level down to pick those up; `buildAllSessions` only ever read the immediate project directory, so on the file backend (`MINDER_USE_DB=0`, and the fallback whenever the index isn't ready) those sessions and all their turns, tokens and cost were simply absent — **1,256 of 4,895 sessions** on the reference machine. This understated totals, `byModel`, `byProject`, `byCategory` and `byEffort` alike, and is a pre-existing divergence rather than a new one: it became visible only because `byEntrypoint` made the shortfall legible, since subagent transcripts inherit their parent's entrypoint and are overwhelmingly `cli`, so the loss landed on one bucket instead of spreading evenly. The reader now walks the nested directories, attributing each file to the **project** dir (not `subagents`) and keying the session on the file's own basename — matching the reconciler exactly. The dual-backend parity fixture gained a nested transcript, without which it could not tell the two readers apart; that omission is why the divergence survived the original parity test.
- **An older build could silently revert an index written by a newer one, destroying columns no re-parse at its version could rebuild.** The ingest staleness gates compared `sessions.derived_version === DERIVED_VERSION` — an equality that is false in *both* directions, so rows stamped **above** the running build's version were indistinguishable from stale ones and were "refreshed" downward, dropping every column the newer build knew about. This is not theoretical: a completed v14 re-parse wrote 4,894 sessions carrying 22,682 `turns.effort` values and 1,141 `task_outcome` stamps — a 45-minute rebuild — and after a tray packaged 2026-08-03 (`DERIVED_VERSION = 12`) started roughly thirty minutes later, the index was found holding 5,001 sessions all stamped 12 with every one of those columns empty. A downgrade reports `errors: 0`, because from the older build's point of view the pass succeeded. Any install running an older packaged app against an index a newer checkout had written was exposed, and the symptom is misleading: it presents as a just-shipped feature rendering nothing, which reads as a UI bug and sends you looking in the wrong layer.

  Both reconcile paths (Claude and the Codex/Gemini adapters) now leave a session untouched when its stored derivation outranks the running build's. Derivation versions only move forward, so a higher stored value means those rows came from a build that knows strictly more; leaving them alone costs nothing, since the newer build re-derives on its next sweep, while rewriting them loses data irrecoverably. The guard sits **before** the tail-append-versus-full-replace decision, not inside the unchanged-file skip gate: that gate requires mtime *and* size to match, so only idle sessions ever reached it — active sessions grew, missed it, failed the version equality that gates tail-append, and fell through to full-replace. A fix applied only to the skip gate passes the obvious test and still destroys the same data, which is covered by a dedicated regression test. `force` deliberately still overrides, so a genuinely rolled-back install can re-derive rather than being stuck with rows it can never rewrite; none of the watcher's three call sites pass it, so this cannot fire on the automatic path. The condition is now counted (`newerDerivationSkips`) and logged once per pass rather than being inferable only from an empty panel.

  **The prune pass needed the same rule.** Guarding only the reconcile gates left an older build able to *delete* newer rows instead of rewriting them, which is worse. The reachable path is a rollback across a build that added an adapter: `getEnabledAdapters` skips an unknown configured id with only a `console.warn`, so discovery *succeeds* having found none of that adapter's files — the existing shield-on-discovery-failure never engages, those sessions are absent from the live-path set, and they look exactly like vanished files. The prune now leaves a session alone when its stored derivation outranks the running build, deferring to the newer build that can actually tell the difference; same-version rows still prune normally, so the index cannot grow forever, and `force` still prunes.
- **Cost reporting understated prompt-cache writes by ~15% of total spend.** Anthropic bills a cache write at 1.25× base input for the default 5-minute TTL and **2× for the 1-hour TTL**, but `ModelPricing` carried a single `cacheWriteCostPerToken` and every cache-write token was priced at the 5-minute rate. That is not an edge case for this tool's primary subject: **Claude Code writes its prompt cache at the 1-hour TTL**, and across the full local corpus (3,726 transcripts, 126,962 priced assistant turns) 95.5% of all cache-creation tokens — 1.12 B of 1.17 B — were 1-hour writes. Re-pricing that history moves reported spend from $27,756 to $31,895, a **$4,139 (14.9%) understatement**. The per-TTL split has been available all along in each turn's `usage.cache_creation` object (`ephemeral_1h_input_tokens` / `ephemeral_5m_input_tokens`) and is now read by all three JSONL readers — the usage parser, the SQLite ingest path, and the conversation scanner — via one shared `usage/cacheTtl.ts` extractor, then billed as two portions by `applyPricing`. LiteLLM already published the second rate as `cache_creation_input_token_cost_above_1hr`; `parseLiteLLMEntry` simply never read it, so the online path needed no new data source. Both new fields are **optional**, which is what makes this safe for every other caller: a turn with no breakdown (older transcripts, the Codex/Gemini adapters) prices exactly as before rather than silently reporting zero 1-hour writes, and a provider with a single write rate keeps flat cache-write pricing. `cacheCreate1hTokens` is carried on the in-memory turn only and deliberately **not** added as a `turns` column — the derived `cost_usd` it produces is the thing worth persisting, and a column would mean a migration for a value nothing queries. **Stored per-turn costs are not retroactively corrected by this change**; they are recomputed on the next full re-parse.
- **Offline pricing billed every modern Opus model at triple its real rate.** The hardcoded fallback table — used whenever LiteLLM and its disk cache are both unavailable, and by `getModelPricing` before `loadPricing()` resolves — stopped at the Claude 4 generation, and model ids nest: the progressive-shortening lookup walked `claude-opus-4-8` down to the `claude-opus-4` key and charged $15/$75 for a model that costs $5/$25. Opus 4.5 and later, Sonnet 5, Fable 5, and Haiku 4.5 are now in the table with their published rates, and generation resolution runs through an explicitly ordered most-specific-first matcher (`claude-opus-4-8` before `claude-opus-4`) rather than a substring walk that could land on an older generation. The keyword fallback for an unversioned or future id now inherits the **newest** known rates for its family instead of the oldest, so a `claude-opus-6` is approximated by Opus 5 rather than by a retired generation. Claude Sonnet 5 is entered at its standard $3/$15 rather than the introductory $2/$10 that runs through 2026-08-31, because LiteLLM tracks the live rate on the online path and the durable number is the useful one in a fallback.
- **Context-window sizes reported 200k for every model, including the 1M ones.** `FALLBACK_MAX_CONTEXT` predated the 1M window and its catch-all returned 200k, so an Opus 5 turn's context fill read as 5× its true proportion — an error in the direction that makes context pressure look safe when it is not. This feeds `max_context_fill`, the burn HUD, per-turn context attribution, and the efficiency grades. Everything from Claude 4.6 onward now reports 1M; Opus 4.5, Sonnet 4.5 and earlier, and Haiku 4.5 correctly stay at 200k. This uses its own ordered table rather than reusing the pricing one, because the two group models differently — Opus 4.5 through Opus 5 share one price but split across the 200k/1M boundary.
- **A single pricing rule silently disabled long-context pricing.** `applyPricingOverlay` constructed a fresh four-field object, so `inputCostPerTokenAbove200k` and `outputCostPerTokenAbove200k` were dropped from any model an override touched — the >200k tier stopped applying entirely for that model. The overlay now spreads the base pricing before applying overrides, so optional rates a rule cannot express survive it. A **Cache Write $/M** override additionally scales the 1-hour rate by the same ratio rather than leaving 1-hour writes at the provider's untouched price.
- **`daily_costs` re-priced turns from token columns instead of summing the cost already computed.** The rollup carried its own copy of the cost formula, which would now diverge from `sessions.cost_usd` — the cache-TTL split it needs is not persisted as a token column. It sums `turns.cost_usd` instead, which is the authoritative per-turn figure and what session totals already sum. (The comment claiming cost "can't be summed in pure SQL because pricing is held in JS" stopped being true when that column landed in schema v3.) `computeCostFromPerModel` in the conversation scanner — and its duplicate inlined in `scanSessionFile` — likewise hand-rolled the formula and had silently missed every refinement `applyPricing` gained, including the >200k tier; both now delegate to it. Delegating required making the tier explicit: the above-200k rates are a per-**request** tier selected by one prompt's size, so handing `applyPricing` a per-model bucket that sums many turns would have read the combined input as a single enormous prompt and billed every ordinary turn in a long session at the long-context rate. The scanner now assigns each turn to a `base` or `long` bucket as it reads it and prices the two separately, and `applyPricing` takes an explicit `PricingTier` so an aggregate caller must state its tier rather than have one inferred from a sum.

## [1.7.0] - 2026-08-03

### Added
- **Settings → Server Port: a way to record a preferred port and see how to apply it.** Port 4100 can land inside a Windows-administered TCP port exclusion range reserved by Hyper-V/WSL2/Docker's WinNAT networking stack — not a conflicting app, an OS-level block that can appear unpredictably after a reboot. The new section stores a `port` preference in `.minder.json` and shows the currently-serving port alongside it, but does not auto-apply anything: a running server can't safely rebind its own listening port mid-request, so it renders copy-paste commands for whichever run mode is in use (`-p <port>` for dev/start, `MINDER_PORT` for the Windows service installer, `MINDER_TRAY_PORT` for the tray app) and links to the Tray App help doc's WinNAT explanation.
- **The embedding index can now keep itself current, on a separate opt-in.** A built index goes stale the moment new sessions are indexed — those chunks have no vectors and are invisible to semantic search until someone presses Build again. The default-off `semanticAutoBackfill` flag tops the index up on the background dispatcher tick instead. It is a **second** switch rather than a behaviour change to `semanticSearch` because it is a different kind of consent: Build spends CPU because you pressed it, this spends CPU with nobody watching. It gates on both flags — topping up an index nothing queries is pure waste — and the Settings toggle disables itself, with an explanation, when semantic search is off, rather than presenting a switch that silently does nothing. The scheduling rules: it runs **only while no agent task is in flight**, so a background nicety can never compete for CPU with the work the dispatcher exists to do; the pass is **not awaited** by the tick, because `tickInProgress` gates the whole tick and waiting on a pass — seconds normally, far longer while the model first loads — would delay task dispatch behind it; and it backs off by how likely the condition is to change on its own, ~10 min when there is nothing to embed, ~20 min after a failure, ~1 hour when the model or the chunk corpus is missing, since those need an install or a migration rather than a retry. The per-tick budget is **250 chunks** (~3.8 s, a ~12% duty cycle), sized for drift rather than bulk — covering a cold corpus this way takes hours, which is the correct speed for unattended work. Two failure modes are closed by construction: a pass that embedded nothing without reporting a stop code is classified as *idle* rather than progress (treating it as progress would reschedule immediately and spin at tick rate), and `running` is cleared on every exit path including a throw, because leaving it set would disable self-heal for the life of the process, silently and permanently. Policy lives in a pure `selfHeal.ts` with 20 tests; the dispatcher only holds the state. A further 8 integration tests drive the real dispatcher tick against a mocked backfill, because a policy module with no caller is exactly the failure this repo has shipped before — they assert the pass fires when both flags are on, never fires when either is off, resumes after a rejecting pass rather than latching off, and does not block the tick while one hangs.
- **Settings → Semantic Search: a panel for the embedding index, so the feature is drivable without curl.** The flag toggle already existed, but enabling it populated no vectors — the backfill was reachable only through `POST /api/embeddings`, which is why the flag shipped off with a "no panel yet" caveat written into its own help doc. The panel adds a coverage bar (`embedded / total` chunks), a remaining-time estimate from the rate actually observed rather than the reference constant, and a **Build index** control that runs bounded passes back to back until the corpus is covered. The driver deliberately lives **in the page**, not on the server: the route's own design note says a loop that decides on its own to spend forty minutes of CPU is not something a local dashboard should do silently, so the run is visible, stoppable mid-flight, ends when you navigate away, and resumes from wherever it stopped. Continuation is gated on **strict forward progress** — a pass must have embedded something *and* left strictly fewer chunks remaining than the pass before it — which makes termination a property of the rule rather than of an arbitrary iteration cap, and turns a server that reported progress it didn't make into a halt instead of an unbounded POST loop. Three reporting fixes came out of building it: corpus-wide progress is derived as `total - remaining` (the response's `embedded` counts *that pass*, so using it directly pinned the bar at one pass forever); a missing chunk corpus is now reported as an index problem with its own remedy instead of being blamed on the model (`indexReady` split out from `available`); and "the runtime hasn't been loaded yet" — the normal state after **every** server restart, since reading coverage deliberately never triggers the download — is no longer rendered as a failure, which it was for any UI reading `available` as a bare boolean. `GET /api/embeddings` also now reports whether model files are already on disk, so the panel can say plainly whether pressing Build will fetch 80 MB. Coverage never rounds up to "100%" while chunks remain. At full coverage the control becomes **Verify index** and stays enabled rather than greying out — `countEmbedded` counts vector rows and `selectUnembedded` joins on chunk keys alone, neither consulting `text_hash`, so a session re-ingested with edited text under the same keys still reports `remaining: 0` while its vectors describe text that no longer exists; the hash check that catches this lives inside a backfill pass, so disabling the button at 100% would have made stale vectors unreachable and left them returning confidently wrong hits. 28 tests.
- **Optional local embeddings: session search can now match on meaning, not just on words you can spell.** A third retriever embeds the query with `all-MiniLM-L6-v2` (384-dim ONNX, run locally — nothing leaves the machine) and feeds its ranking into the Reciprocal Rank Fusion that #1 built. That fusion layer was designed for exactly this: its own documentation already names "no embedding model" as a retriever that must drop out gracefully, and because RRF consumes ordinal position only, a cosine similarity and a BM25 score never have to be put on a common scale. The gap it closes is concrete — a session that says "database migration error" does not surface for the query "the migration is failing" under keyword search, and in an end-to-end run against real transcript text the top three hits for that query contained the word "migration" **zero** times. Every number here is measured rather than estimated, deliberately: an earlier sizing exercise in this repo was extrapolated from a small sample and came out wrong by 2.6x. Vectors are **int8-quantized**: at 157 146 chunks, float32 would add 241 MB to an 834 MB database (a 29% increase for a default-off feature) while int8 adds **58 MB** (7%), confirmed at 384 bytes per vector on disk. Quantization is safe because the model is asked for L2-normalized output, which makes the scale fixed and cosine a plain dot product; rank order is preserved across meaningfully separated similarities, and the near-tie reordering it *does* cause is pinned as a test rather than papered over. Search is **brute force**, and that is a measurement, not a shrug: a complete scan of the full corpus takes **162 ms**, the same order as the FTS query beside it, so an ANN index would buy latency that isn't the bottleneck at the price of a second native dependency. Vectors are keyed on `(session_id, turn_index, chunk_index)` — the identity `schema.sql` documents — rather than on `prompts_fts`'s rowid, which is reassigned whenever the writer rebuilds it and would silently re-associate every vector with different text after a re-index. The backfill is **budgeted and resumable**, because embedding an existing corpus is ~40 minutes of CPU: each call embeds a bounded batch and returns, **newest chunks first**, so recent sessions become searchable long before the pass finishes. `@huggingface/transformers` is an **optional dependency** with the same posture as `better-sqlite3` — a missing package, a failed download, an empty index, or a mid-query error all yield an empty list, and an empty list contributes nothing to the fusion, so search degrades to keyword-only instead of erroring. The model cache is redirected to `~/.minder/models/` because the library's default puts it inside `node_modules`, where the next `pnpm install` would silently delete 80 MB and re-download it. Default-off `semanticSearch` flag: turning it on is the consent for the download and the backfill. 42 tests.
- **Quota-aware dispatch: the task queue now waits out a rate limit instead of failing into it.** `quota.ts` has read the authoritative `anthropic-ratelimit-unified-*` headers — including a real per-window `resetAt` — since the burn HUD shipped, and `tasks/dispatcher.ts` has spawned queued work; they had never been connected. The dispatcher does not retry, so a task claimed while the window is exhausted spawned straight into the wall, failed, and the work was gone. The gate turns a lost task into a late one: while a window is exhausted the queue holds, and the first tick after the reset (~30 s) picks it back up. **The queue is held, not rescheduled.** Nothing is written to `ops_tasks.scheduled_for`: stamping a reset time onto every pending row would mean owning the un-stamping too, would strand the queue behind a stale deadline if quota recovered early or the reading was wrong, and would overwrite a `scheduled_for` the user set themselves. Rows stay `pending`; the next tick claims them. **The hold runs to the latest reset among exhausted windows**, not the earliest — releasing at the 5-hour reset while the 7-day window is also exhausted would send the queue directly back into the wall.
