The utilization threshold is **0.98** rather than 1.0, because a long task started at 99% hits the limit mid-run and dies with its work unsaved, while the cost of holding is bounded by `resetAt`. **Every unclear case fails open**, which is the property the whole design turns on: no hold when quota is unconfigured, when the reading is older than 30 minutes (`loadQuota()` falls back to a disk cache when its probe fails, so a reading can be arbitrarily old and an hour-old "throttled" would otherwise hold the queue against a window that reset long ago), when the reading is future-dated, when an exhausted window carries no reset time, when the reset has already passed, or when it is more than 8 days out. A gate that holds on bad data silently stops all background work — strictly worse than one visible failed task. While held, the state rides in the dispatcher heartbeat, in `GET /api/tasks` under `dispatcher.quotaHold`, and as a **Dispatch paused** notice in the task composer naming the resume time. New default-on `quotaAwareDispatch` flag. 19 tests.
- **Cross-harness config drift detection: what Claude has that Codex doesn't, and vice versa.** A new `Cross-harness Drift` target in Config Lint compares MCP servers, skills, and instruction files across Claude Code, Codex, and Gemini CLI, reporting what exists in one harness and not another. This is the deliberate **inversion** of ai-devkit, which reconciles `CLAUDE.md` / `AGENTS.md` / `.cursor/rules` / MCP config by *generating* them from one manifest: Minder never writes harness config, because the filesystem is the source of truth and the index is derived from it — becoming a writer would break that invariant. Three decisions make the output usable rather than merely correct. **Gaps are summarized, conflicts are per-item.** On a real machine Claude carries 57 user skills and Codex carries 8; emitting one finding per missing skill produces ~49 rows nobody reads and buries the two that matter, so a one-directional gap becomes a single finding naming a count and five examples (with the overflow count always stated). Conflicts — the same MCP server name pointed at a different command in two harnesses, where one side is plainly stale — stay per-item, because those are few and each is actionable. **Root instruction files are compared as one artifact under three names:** `CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md`, and `GEMINI.md` play the same role, so a filename match would report each as permanently missing from the other two harnesses forever. **Symlinked entries are followed:** `readdir({withFileTypes})` reports a link as `isSymbolicLink()` and never `isDirectory()`, and 24 of 57 skills under `~/.claude/skills` on the development machine are stow-style links — dropping them would be worse than undercounting, since an entry missing from an *inventory* is reported as missing from that *harness*, i.e. the tool telling you to install what you already have. Every finding is **P2**: divergence between harnesses is frequently deliberate, and `LintReport.hasBlocking` is documented as the strict-lint gate, so enabling a second adapter must not be able to fail someone's CI on a parity observation. The pass is silent — and does no filesystem work at all — unless a second adapter is enabled in Settings and its config home exists, which the default `enabledAdapters` of `["claude"]` never satisfies. New default-on `configDrift` flag. 30 tests.
- **Session → Markdown export now reads the transcript instead of the index, and says so when it can't.** `GET /api/sessions/<id>/export` renders a whole session as one self-contained markdown document — YAML front matter, a heading per message, collapsible `<details>` for extended thinking and tool results — at three detail levels (`minimal` / `standard` / `full`), with per-toggle overrides for thinking, tool calls, tool results, and subagent messages. The load-bearing decision is **where the text comes from**. Every in-memory session abstraction in this repo is a preview: `SessionDetail.timeline` caps assistant text at 300 characters on the file-parse path and 500 on the DB path, and `UsageTurn.assistantText` caps at 500 — they exist to render lists and feed search, not to reproduce a conversation. An export built on them cuts every substantial message mid-sentence while looking complete, so the exporter reads the session's own JSONL (`src/lib/sessions/exportReader.ts`) and only the metadata comes from the index. When the JSONL genuinely isn't there — pruned by Claude Code's retention, synced from another machine, over the 50 MB ceiling, or demo mode — the export **degrades rather than 404s**, falling back to the index and declaring it twice: `fidelity: "index"` in the front matter and a warning callout at the top of the document, because the difference is otherwise invisible from inside the file. Two formatting properties are enforced rather than hoped for. **Fences are sized to their content:** a tool result containing ` ``` ` closes a three-backtick wrapper at the transcript's own fence and renders the entire remainder of the document as prose, so every emitted fence exceeds the longest backtick run inside it (CommonMark permits arbitrary-length fences). **Truncation is always announced:** each cut block carries an inline `… truncated N characters` marker and the document ends with an `Export notes` line tallying blocks omitted by the detail level, subagent messages excluded, and characters lost — a silent cap would make a partial export read as a complete one. The renderer (`src/lib/sessions/markdownExport.ts`) is pure and takes its `exportedAt` stamp as an argument, so two renders of the same session are byte-identical. Front-matter scalars are JSON-quoted, which is what keeps a Windows `project_path` and a project name containing `:` or `#` valid YAML. 53 tests.
- **Notification rules: an open-ended `{field, operator, pattern} → channels` engine replacing a closed two-event enum.** `notificationPrefs.events` was a literal object type with exactly two keys, validated against a hardcoded `LIVE_EVENT_KEYS` array — so every new alert meant editing the type, the validator, the dispatcher, and the Settings UI, and a user could not express an alert we had not anticipated. Rules are evaluated in `/api/hooks` against every live hook event, which is what makes the interesting alerts reachable at all: **a session reading `.env` arrives as a `PreToolUse`, not as a notification**, so no amount of widening the old event enum would have caught it. Six presets ship (`.env` access, `bypassPermissions` mode, failed tool call, `rm -rf`, `git push --force`, slow tool call), every one of them using `contains`/`equals`/`gt` rather than `regex` — presets are then safe by construction and unaffected by the regex policy below. Across a 60-project tree the `.env` rule is the one that earns the feature: it reports the moment a session touches credentials, whether or not you asked it to. Several decisions are load-bearing rather than incidental. **The field namespace is curated, not arbitrary JSON paths.** A rule written against `tool_input.file_path` silently stops matching when Claude Code renames a wire field — and silently is the whole problem, because a security alert that has quietly stopped firing is worse than no alert. `fields.ts` absorbs upstream shape changes in one place, and every field is length-capped there, which is what makes user-authored regex affordable on a request path. **Tool-input leaves are capped individually (512 chars each) rather than only in total.** An `Edit` carries a multi-kilobyte `new_string` beside the `file_path` that actually matters; a total-only cap lets one giant leaf push every other key past the truncation point, so a `.env` rule would match a small edit and miss a large one — a size-dependent false negative, which is the worst failure shape for this feature. A test pins `.env` still matching behind a 30 KB edit body. **Absent fields never match.** A `prompt` rule is skipped on a tool call rather than tested against an empty string, so `equals ""`-style accidents cannot fire on every event. The corollary is that a field which is *conceptually* present must be emitted even when falsy: `tool.failed` is set to `"false"` on every successful `PostToolUse` rather than omitted, because the route leaves its `toolFailed` flag undefined on success, and omitting it would make `tool.failed equals false` unsatisfiable while the editor and the help page both offer it. Success is a fact the event carries, not a missing value. **The `os` channel is delivered through the pulse stream** (`/api/pulse` → `NotificationListener`), reusing the edge-triggered drain `awaiting-permission` already uses, because an OS notification can only be raised by a browser holding permission — the server cannot send one, so a match has to survive until a tab asks for it. Queuing it is not optional: `os` is the default channel for a new custom rule and the only channel on two presets, so treating it as "already handled elsewhere" left precisely those rules silently doing nothing. The queue is bounded at 50 and drops oldest-first, since with no tab open nothing consumes it and a stale alert is worth less than a current one. **Cooldown is per rule *and* per project, and is distinct from payload dedup.** The existing 5-minute window suppresses *identical* notifications; a failing test loop produces a *different* error string on each retry, so dedup alone would have sent one notification per attempt. Cooldown state is a `globalThis` singleton for the same reason the git-status and GitHub caches are — dev-mode module reloading would otherwise hand each request a fresh Map and make every rule fire every time — and its eviction drops the oldest half rather than clearing, because a `clear()` resets every live cooldown at once and turns a memory bound into a notification storm. Per-project keying means one noisy repo cannot mute the same rule elsewhere. **Evaluation is fire-and-forget and cannot throw.** A Claude Code process is blocked on the hook response, so the engine is wrapped in a single `try`/`catch` and the route never awaits delivery; matching itself is linear in the capped field length. **Validation lives in a pure module called by `/api/config`, and the matcher re-checks the limits it depends on rather than trusting it**, because `.minder.json` is user-editable and a rule can reach the engine without ever passing through the API. Rules replace wholesale on write rather than merging — the opposite of the merge semantics the same route needs elsewhere — because a merge makes deleting a rule impossible. **Two claude-devtools features are deliberately not shipped.** Token-usage thresholds are absent because hook payloads carry no token counts: that needs a polling evaluator over the usage index, and inventing a field the data cannot fill would have been worse than its absence, so the docs say so explicitly. Arbitrary-transcript-field regex is served by the `any` field (every text field concatenated, separately capped) rather than by exposing raw transcript paths, for the stability reason above. **User-authored regex is screened for superlinear worst cases before it is ever compiled**, because the field cap alone is not a defence: JavaScript's engine backtracks and cannot be interrupted, there is no timeout to fall back on, and `(a+)+$` against 4 000 characters does not finish in the lifetime of the process — so an unsafe pattern is not a slow rule but an unkillable hang of the receiver, and therefore of the session blocked on it. Two shapes are refused: a quantifier applied to a group that itself quantifies or alternates (exponential), and two *adjacent* unbounded quantifiers whose atoms can match the same character (`.*.*`, `\w+\w+`, `\w+\d+` — polynomial, and invisible to the group rule since no group is involved). The second test compares **character sets, not spelling**: `\w+\d+$` has textually different atoms, but every digit is also a word character, so a run of digits still splits between them quadratically — measured at ~22s against a non-matching 4 000-character field, which is long enough to stall the receiver. Overlap is decided by evaluating both atoms against every printable ASCII character rather than a sampled set of equivalence classes, because sampling is unsound in the dangerous direction: `[a-f]+[d-z]*` intersects only on `d`–`f`, so any probe set missing those letters waves it through. The cost is irrelevant — it runs only when a pattern actually contains two adjacent unbounded quantifiers, and the verdict is cached. The screen is deliberately strict and does reject some safe patterns — `(foo|bar)+` cannot actually backtrack, since the alternatives do not overlap, but no cheap syntactic check can prove that. Strict is the cheaper error: a rejected pattern costs one confusing "my rule never fires", an accepted bad one costs a frozen editor, and the workaround (lift the quantifier off the group) is mechanical and documented. Strictness is bounded where it would otherwise become useless — the adjacency rule fires only for *identical* atoms or a `.`, because the naive "two unbounded quantifiers in a row" version also rejects `a*b+`, `\w+\s*`, and `[a-z]+[0-9]*`, which are disjoint, safe, and ubiquitous. Both refusals are reported at the `/api/config` boundary with an actionable message rather than silently producing a rule that never fires, and syntax is checked *before* safety so an ordinary typo is not reported as "unsafe" — `([` is an unterminated class, which the safety scan also rejects, and calling that "unsafe" would be true but useless to whoever has to fix it. Checking syntax first is free: building a regex is linear and cannot backtrack; only running one can, so the execution path keeps the safety guard while the syntax probe discards its result. The matcher re-checks safety independently for patterns hand-written straight into `.minder.json`, where they never pass the API at all; there a refusal degrades to a rule that does not fire, which is the only option left once no one is listening for an error. Every catastrophic shape, every accepted realistic pattern, and every knowingly-sacrificed safe pattern is pinned by test, so the cost side of the trade stays visible rather than being rediscovered. Adapted from [claude-devtools](https://github.com/matt1398/claude-devtools).

- **New default-off `Tool approval gate`: hold a tool call at a `PreToolUse` hook until you approve it in Minder.** Minder's existing `/api/hooks` receiver is fire-and-forget — it records lifecycle events and returns immediately — so it could notify you that Claude was waiting for permission but could not answer for you. `POST /api/hooks/permission` is the blocking counterpart: it holds the request open until a human decides via `POST /api/approvals/[id]`, and writes the answer back as the hook's stdout, which is how Claude Code's synchronous `PreToolUse` hook receives a decision. `GET /api/approvals` lists what is currently blocked. Three properties are load-bearing, and each is a deliberate decision rather than an implementation detail. **Fail open, always** — every failure path (deadline passed, server not running, unreadable payload, internal throw, feature disabled) returns `permissionDecision: "ask"`, which is exactly Claude Code's normal permission flow. The gate never produces a `deny` on its own; the absence of an answer is not a no. A monitoring tool that can wedge the thing it monitors is worse than no monitoring tool, so the whole route body sits inside one `try`/`catch` whose handler still returns a usable decision, and the curl command carries `--max-time` (a few seconds above the server's own deadline, so the server normally wins the race) plus `-f`, which makes any HTTP error produce empty stdout — read by Claude Code as "the hook expressed no opinion" rather than as a parse error. **Replay-safe** — a decision is honoured only while that exact request is still pending; deciding twice, deciding after a timeout, or replaying a captured id all no-op and return 409. Without this, a stale phone notification tapped ten minutes later could approve a *different* tool call that happened to be waiting, so the failure mode being prevented is not a wasted tap but the wrong thing being approved. **Bounded** — entries expire on a deadline and are swept, so a hook that dies mid-request (terminal closed, machine slept) cannot leak an entry forever; the registry is memory-only by design, since a pending approval is meaningless once the process holding the hook is gone and persisting it would only resurrect dead prompts. Read-only tools (`Read`, `Grep`, `Glob`, `LS`, `NotebookRead`, `TodoRead`) bypass the round-trip, via an **allowlist rather than a denylist** — the polarity is the safety property, because a denylist would mean every tool added to Claude Code in future, and every tool on every connected MCP server, silently skipped the gate with nothing surfacing that it happened. `WebFetch` and `WebSearch` are deliberately **not** bypassed despite not mutating local state: they egress, and a URL can carry data off the machine. That is a considered departure from claude-pulse, which passes all read-only tools through. `mcp__*` tools can never be bypassed, since `mcp__github__create_pull_request` reads like a query to any name-based heuristic and is not. A bypass returns `ask` rather than `allow`, so skipping Minder's gate does not also skip the user's own Claude Code permission settings. "Allow all" is scoped to a single session rather than global, so one unattended run cannot disarm the gate everywhere. Approval summaries are length-capped because they reach notification transports, where a 40 KB `Write` payload would be useless at best and a way to push file content somewhere unintended at worst. Adapted from [claude-pulse](https://github.com/nikitadoudikov/claude-pulse), whose fail-open design, read-only bypass, and replay guard are the three ideas worth copying verbatim — but **not** its transport: Pulse routes approval prompts through the public ntfy.sh relay and embeds its bearer token in the notification payload, so this ships as localhost/LAN only. **The hook is registered by `installLiveActivityHooks`, as a second `PreToolUse` entry rather than a replacement for the existing one** — the fire-and-forget entry is what records tool activity for the dashboard, and dropping it to make room would trade one feature for another. Registration is unconditional and independent of the feature flag, because installation happens once while the flag is toggled later: gating the install would mean a user who flips the toggle in Settings gets no gate at all until they reinstall hooks. The route evaluates the flag per request instead, so a registered-but-disabled hook costs one localhost round-trip and changes nothing else. The idempotency check for that entry is deliberately narrower than the one guarding the lifecycle entries (`isApprovalCommand`, not `isManagedCommand`) and the two commands carry distinct User-Agent sentinels: anyone who installed hooks before this feature existed already has a managed `PreToolUse` entry, so the coarser test would skip the event and leave the gate permanently unreachable, with a clean install being the only configuration where it appeared to work. `getLiveActivityHookStatus` reports `approvalHookRegistered` separately from `installed` for the same reason — the former is the only one that answers "will the flag do anything?". Three timeouts are nested and their order is load-bearing: the server's decide deadline (60s) < curl's `--max-time` (63s) < Claude Code's own hook timeout (70s), so each outer layer is a backstop for the one inside it. Reversing any pair means Claude reaps the hook mid-wait and the gate silently allows far less time to decide than it was configured with, which presents as approvals timing out at random. Deadline bounds are clamped in the store, next to the `setTimeout` they constrain rather than only at the route, since the value arrives in the hook payload from outside — a lower bound matters too, because a negative value passes `Number.isFinite` and would fire the deadline immediately, making the gate look broken rather than fast. Concurrently blocked calls are capped at 50, which is what makes the registry bounded in *count* as well as in time; past the cap requests fail open rather than queueing, since a queued call would block its tool while being invisible to whoever is meant to answer it. The receiver checks the approval sentinel and answers `ask` to anything else — 200-with-`ask` rather than a 403, preserving the every-response-is-a-valid-hook-payload invariant while still refusing to let a stray local caller create a pending entry and its notification. Notifications resolve the payload's `cwd` to a real project slug through the shared resolver `/api/hooks` already uses, because `dispatchAwaitingPermission` builds its deep link as `/project/<slug>`: passing the Claude session id through produced a notification that opened a route which does not exist.
- **Session search now reads whole messages, extended thinking, and subagent transcripts — not just the first 500 characters.** `prompts_fts` mirrored `turns.text_preview`, a 500-character preview, so a keyword at character 3000 of an assistant reply was unsearchable and the search reported nothing rather than an error. The index now holds full turn bodies split into overlapping ~4000-character chunks (`src/lib/db/textChunks.ts`), one FTS row per chunk. Three things become findable that never were: **the body of long replies**, **extended-thinking content** (thinking blocks were parsed only to set the `has_thinking` flag and their text was then discarded, so "why did Claude choose this approach" could not be searched at all), and **subagent transcripts** (sidechain rows carry no preview column, so any work delegated to a Task agent was invisible to search at any length — a large blind spot given how much work runs through subagents). Chunking rather than one-row-per-turn is a ranking requirement, not just a size convenience: FTS5's bm25 normalizes by document length, so a 40 KB turn and a 200-character turn indexed as single documents are not comparably rankable and the long one is systematically penalized; uniform chunks keep document length roughly constant. The chunks overlap because a fixed stride cuts phrases in half — split "the migration is failing" across a boundary and the phrase exists in neither chunk, so a phrase query silently never matches; a 400-character overlap guarantees any shorter phrase survives intact in at least one chunk, which makes the overlap a correctness bound on the longest reliably-matchable phrase rather than a tuning knob. **Tool inputs and outputs are deliberately excluded.** They are roughly 60% of total transcript volume (231 MB of 380 MB measured across the corpus) and are mostly grep output, file dumps, and JSON payloads — high index cost, low search signal, and the underlying files are on disk and greppable anyway. **Measured cost, after building it: `prompts_fts` grows 81.5 MB → ~233 MB and `index.db` 682 MB → ~834 MB (+22%)** — from reconciling a 103-file stride sample of the real 2658-file corpus into a throwaway index. That is roughly 2.6x cheaper than the ~1.22 GB projected before implementation, and the discrepancy is worth recording because the projection method was subtly wrong rather than merely imprecise: the 4.12x storage-overhead ratio it used was measured accurately from the live index, but that index holds 154,097 rows averaging ~128 bytes of text, where fixed per-row bookkeeping dominates. Applying that ratio to a larger text volume implicitly assumed overhead scales with bytes; it largely scales with row count. Chunking produces fewer, fatter rows (2,166 rows for 4,314 turns — 0.50 chunks per turn, since tool-result-only and tool_use-only turns contribute nothing under tier B), so the fixed cost amortizes over ~2,585 characters instead of ~128 and the real overhead is 1.6x. Tool I/O was still the right thing to exclude — it is 60% of the volume for the least searchable content — but it would have cost roughly ~600 MB rather than the ~1 GB feared. **Scope limit:** Codex and Gemini sessions remain preview-indexed. Their adapters cap text at 500 characters (`adapters/utils.ts` `TEXT_CAP`) before ingest ever sees a turn, so full text is not available on that path at all; lifting this requires widening the `SessionAdapter` contract and was deliberately left out. Those sessions are indexed exactly as well as before — no regression, just no improvement. The new index is populated by a one-time full re-parse driven by `DERIVED_VERSION` 12; until it completes, prompt-scope search returns fewer hits than it will afterwards. That is degraded rather than wrong — title-scope search is unaffected and nothing returned during the window is incorrect — and was chosen over a read-side gate, which would have made search return nothing at all during catch-up.
- **New per-session `Context` tab showing what actually filled the context window, turn by turn.** Minder could already estimate context overhead *before* a session starts (`contextOverhead.ts` on `/stats` — a static, portfolio-wide figure derived from MCP server, skill, and memory file sizes, identical for every session). It could not answer the retrospective question: on turn 47, where did the 90k go? The new tab breaks each session's window contribution into six sources — your prompts, attached context, Claude's replies, extended thinking, tool inputs, and tool output — with a whole-session composition bar, a per-turn contribution strip, and a headline naming the dominant source ("Tool output accounted for 71% of the context this session put into the window"). **The series is segmented at compaction boundaries**, because cumulative context is meaningless across one: the window is discarded and rebuilt, so a continuous line would climb straight through a reset that actually happened. Each segment reports its own peak fill, which is what makes "here is where you hit the limit and lost your plan" legible rather than a table of numbers. **What is measured and what is estimated is stated rather than blurred.** Per-turn window totals are ground truth — the API reports `input_tokens`, `cache_read_input_tokens`, and `cache_creation_input_tokens` on every assistant turn. The breakdown is not: nothing in the transcript says "8k of this turn was CLAUDE.md", so attribution is estimated from message sizes at ~4 bytes per token, the same heuristic used elsewhere in the codebase. The difference between the two is surfaced as **unattributed** rather than normalized away — that remainder is the system prompt, tool definitions, CLAUDE.md, and skill bodies, all injected by Claude Code and never present in the transcript. A chart that silently scaled to 100% would look more finished and be worth less. The comparison is made against attribution **as it stood at the moment of peak fill**, not against the segment's running total: `peakMeasuredTokens` is `input_tokens + cache` on one assistant turn — the window as it was fed *in* — so it excludes that turn's own reply, thinking, and tool calls, and every turn after it. Subtracting the whole-segment total would compare two different moments and always understate the remainder by at least the final response, and a large response could floor it to zero and hide the harness overhead entirely. It is still floored at zero, since the estimate is a lower bound and a negative would render as "saved tokens". Two categorization decisions are load-bearing: harness-injected user turns (`<system-reminder>` blocks, `@`-mention expansions) count as **attached context** rather than "your prompts", because they are user-role but not user-authored and lumping them together makes the chart claim you typed tens of thousands of characters you never typed; and **subagent turns are excluded entirely**, because they run in their own context window — their tokens are billed and do appear in usage totals, but folding them into the parent would inflate its apparent fill with tokens that were never in it. Served by `GET /api/sessions/[sessionId]/context-attribution`, which reads the raw JSONL on demand rather than the parsed turn model or the SQLite index: both of those cap text at 500 characters (a deliberate parity contract with the file-parse path) and the DB path additionally drops thinking blocks, so either would produce a chart whose bars were all the same truncated size. One file read per request for one session needs no schema change, no migration, and no `DERIVED_VERSION` bump. Assistant messages are de-duplicated by `message.id` (falling back to `requestId`), matching the canonical parser — Claude Code re-logs a message on retries and on resumed-session re-emit, and counting each copy would inflate turn counts and every category total in exactly the sessions most worth inspecting. Compaction detection covers all four record shapes Claude Code has emitted across CLI versions (the `compact_boundary` subtype, a `compact_summary` entry, a top-level `compactSummary` field, and the older marker embedded in system-message text), since missing one causes the report to silently accumulate across a reset that really happened. The tab is shown for Claude sessions only: Codex and Gemini transcripts live outside the directory this endpoint reads, so it is hidden rather than rendered as a guaranteed 404. Adapted from [claude-devtools](https://github.com/matt1398/claude-devtools)' context reconstruction; its "CLAUDE.md", "skills", and "team overhead" buckets are not per-turn observable in Minder's data and are deliberately not invented — they land in `unattributed`.

### Changed
- **The session Export modal's controls are now detail levels rather than section checkboxes.** It previously offered "conversation timeline / file operations / subagents" plus a turn limit, and built the document client-side from `data.timeline` — which meant the output inherited that field's 300/500-character preview cap and emitted no code fences at all, so any session containing ` ``` ` produced a file whose structure collapsed partway through. The modal now picks **Minimal / Standard / Full** (plus an extended-thinking toggle when the session has thinking) and fetches from the export endpoint. File operations and subagents are no longer separate opt-ins: they appear inline as tool calls at Standard and above, in the order they actually happened, which is what a transcript is. Both actions now report what they produced — message count, size, and truncation count — instead of downloading silently.
- **`prompts_fts` is now populated by the writer instead of by triggers (schema v19).** The `turns_ai` / `turns_au` triggers mirrored `turns.text_preview` into the index; they cannot express full-body indexing, because the full body is never stored in `turns` for a trigger to read — only the 500-character preview is. `writeSession` and `appendSessionTail` now chunk the parsed JSONL text and insert FTS rows directly. This continues the direction migration v4 started (writer-owned FTS deletes) and supersedes the file's "FTS5 triggers, not ingest discipline" principle for `prompts_fts` specifically; `catalog_fts` remains fully trigger-driven, because its source tables do store the text they index. The existing session-scoped delete contract is unchanged and still correct — it never keyed on `turn_index`, so it removes every chunk of every turn in one scan. A side benefit: a re-derive UPDATE that rewrites `turns.category` no longer churns FTS rows for text that did not change. The table gains a `chunk_index` column, which required dropping and recreating it since FTS5 virtual tables do not support `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`; dropping is required rather than merely convenient, because leaving 500-character rows mixed in with ~4000-character ones would skew every bm25 ranking under length normalization.
- **Session search now merges its two retrievers by Reciprocal Rank Fusion instead of by comparing incomparable scores.** `GET /api/sessions/search` (and the `search-sessions` MCP tool) draw from two sources whose scores share no scale: `prompts` is FTS5 `bm25()`, an unbounded negative number whose magnitude depends on corpus statistics, while `titles` is a `LIKE` scan that produces no score at all and could only be ordered by recency. The previous implementation squashed bm25 into `[0,1]` via `-rank / (1 + -rank)` and assigned every title hit a hardcoded `0.5` — a constant the code itself described as chosen "to keep mixed-source UNION sortable without claiming spurious precision". Two consequences followed from that constant: every title hit tied with every other title hit, so recency was the only thing separating them and nothing distinguished a slug match from an incidental one; and the 0.5 floor acted as an arbitrary threshold, so any title match outranked any prompt match whose squashed score fell below it while losing to any that fell above — a cutoff with no meaning in either direction. RRF (`src/lib/data/rrf.ts`) discards magnitude entirely and fuses on ordinal position, `score = Σ weight / (k + rank)` with `k = 60`, so agreement between retrievers becomes the dominant signal and no normalization is needed. A session found by both now reliably outranks one found densely by a single retriever. The damping constant is doing real work at this scale rather than being a copied default: the `titles` list is ordered by recency rather than relevance, and `k = 60` compresses rank 1 through rank 20 into a 1.3x spread so those deep, weakly-meaningful ranks cannot dominate. Each retriever is also queried for 3x the requested `limit` (capped at 300) before fusion, because fusion can only reorder what it is given — without the wider pool, a session ranked 55th by keyword but 1st by title would never enter a `limit=50` query at all, which is precisely the cross-retriever rescue RRF exists to perform. Titles keep a 1.5x weight over prompts, preserving as a smooth per-rank preference the intent the old code expressed as a hardcoded floor plus a `>=` tie-break: a match on a slug, project name, or git branch is deliberate, whereas a turn-preview match can be incidental. `SessionSearchHit.score` therefore changes meaning — it is still strictly positive and descending-sortable, but it is no longer a `[0,1]` relevance figure and must not be rendered as a percentage; a lone rank-1 hit now scores ~0.016. No consumer was affected: `SessionsBrowser` reads only `sessionId` to build its filter set, and the MCP tool passes hits through as JSON without arithmetic. Hits additionally carry a diagnostic `ranks: { titles?, prompts? }` giving each retriever's 1-based rank, so "why did this outrank that?" is answerable from a single API response without re-running the query.
### Fixed
- **Semantic search: the orphan sweep was quadratic and the stale sweep never advanced.** All three key columns in `prompts_fts` are `UNINDEXED`, so a `DELETE ... WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM prompts_fts ...)` plans a full scan of the virtual table for *every* vector row — roughly 157 000 × 157 000 comparisons on a fully-embedded corpus, on every backfill pass. It now reads the corpus key set once and compares in memory. The bounded staleness sweep used a bare `LIMIT` with no cursor, so it re-examined the same leading rows on every pass and a chunk that changed outside that window kept its stale vector indefinitely; the offset now rotates and wraps. The help doc also described running the backfill "from Settings", which does not exist — the flag toggle enables the feature but populates nothing, and the panel is still a tracked follow-up. (#361)
- **Semantic search: a stale vector could outlive the text it described.** A session reparse deletes and recreates its `prompts_fts` rows while `(session_id, turn_index, chunk_index)` survives intact, so the backfill treated a recreated chunk as already embedded and left the OLD vector permanently attached to NEW text — semantic search returning confidently wrong sessions with nothing to notice it by. Each vector now stores a hash of the text it was built from (post-truncation, so an edit beyond the model's input window correctly does *not* force a re-embed), and a bounded sweep at the start of each backfill drops mismatches and prunes vectors whose session is gone. Also: `model` was outside the primary key, so `INSERT OR REPLACE` overwrote a chunk's vector when a second model embedded it — the exact opposite of the surrounding comments' claim that old-model rows stay readable during a switch. It is now part of the key, and a table of the older shape is dropped and rebuilt rather than migrated (embeddings are a rebuildable derived artifact, so re-running the backfill costs CPU and nothing else). The `semanticSearch` flag was missing `defaultOn: false`, so Settings rendered the toggle **on** for a fresh config while search and the backfill endpoint both gated on `false` — the feature looked enabled and returned nothing. Cosine scores are clamped to the documented [-1, 1] range, which int8 rounding could otherwise exceed by a hair. (#361)
- **Quota gate: dry-run tasks were held alongside real ones.** A `dry_run` row never starts Claude and never consumes quota — the dispatcher just marks it complete — but the hold sat in front of the whole claim loop, so those safe rows stayed pending for as long as the window was exhausted. While a hold is active the dispatcher now claims only dry-run rows, filtered in SQL: a first attempt moved the check to just before the spawn and requeued held real tasks, which still starved every dry-run sitting behind one, because priority ordering hands back the same row on the next iteration. (#360)
- **Quota gate: an absent status header could have paused the task queue for eight days.** `parseWindow()` writes the literal string `"unknown"` whenever a rate-limit status header is missing, and the gate tested `status !== "allowed"` — so an ordinary incomplete reading was read as throttled, and with a valid future reset beside it the entire default-on queue would hold until that reset. Recognized throttled statuses are now an allowlist; anything unfamiliar falls through to the utilization check, which is the fail-open behaviour the module was documented as having. Two smaller holds on the same feature: an emergency stop returned before the gate ran, freezing a previously recorded hold so the heartbeat and task composer advertised an expired resume time indefinitely; and the heartbeat was only rewritten when a hold was *active*, so a hold that had just cleared kept being advertised for the rest of the tick while dispatch had already resumed. (#360)
- **Cross-harness drift: rule files are now their own kind, and two more read-failure gaps closed.** Gemini has no `rules/` directory, but rules and the root instruction file shared one `instruction` kind — so Gemini participated on the strength of its `GEMINI.md` alone and was then reported as missing every rule Claude and Codex had. Splitting them fixes it. An unreadable or malformed Gemini `settings.json` also still counted as "no MCP servers configured", reporting every Claude and Codex server as missing from it; that now excludes the kind, matching how unreadable directories are already handled. The signature redactor missed `Authorization:` / `--header Authorization: Bearer …` arguments, which is one of the commonest ways an MCP endpoint carries a credential. (#359)
- **Cross-harness drift could have printed MCP credentials on screen.** A conflict finding interpolates each side's launch signature into its text, which `GET /api/drift` returns and Settings renders. Codex signatures arrive via the adapter's `redactConfig`, but Gemini's are read from raw `settings.json` and Claude's from parsed URLs and argv — neither redacted — so an MCP endpoint carrying a token in its query string, or an `--api-key` argument, would have been shown verbatim. Signatures are now redacted (URL userinfo, credential query params, `--key`/`--token` arguments, and prefix-announced secrets like `sk-`/`ghp_`) before leaving the inventory. Three more on the same PR: an unreadable directory (EACCES, a transient FS error) was indistinguishable from an absent one, so a permissions problem made `detectDrift` report every item in the *other* harness as missing — that kind is now excluded from the comparison instead; `rules/` inventoried any non-dotfile, so a stray `notes.json` or editor backup was reported as a missing instruction file (it now uses the same `.md`/`.rules`/`.txt` allowlist as the canonical reader); and a failed `/api/drift` request rendered as "No divergence found — the compared harnesses agree", turning an error into a false clean result. (#359)
- **Cross-harness drift findings rendered nowhere.** They were appended to `ScanResult.catalogLintFindings`, but the Config Lint tab reads `project.configLint`, and the catalog collection only feeds portfolio stats and per-entry badges keyed on a catalog id — which a synthetic `drift:*` key never matches. The feature produced aggregate counts and not one readable finding, while the help doc described where to see them. This is the same failure shape as three prior PRs: documentation promising behaviour the code never wired up. Fixed by putting drift where its subject actually lives — `GET /api/drift` and a panel in **Settings → Adapters**, beside the toggles that decide which harnesses get compared — rather than in a per-project tab it was never project-scoped enough for. Four more findings on the same PR: a `skills/` subdirectory with no `SKILL.md` was inventoried as an installed skill (the canonical walker requires the file), so ordinary folders were reported as missing from other harnesses; Gemini's global context file was hardcoded to `GEMINI.md`, producing a false "root instructions missing" finding for anyone who set `context.fileName`; the Claude inventory resolved `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` for skills and instructions while `getUserConfig()` read MCP servers from `~/.claude` regardless, yielding a mixed inventory with both false gaps and false conflicts (it now matches `getUserConfig()` rather than claiming half-support for an override); and directory listings applied their entry cap to raw `readdir` output before filtering dotfiles or sorting, so a large directory's results depended on filesystem order and could flap between runs. (#359)
- **Session export ignored Codex and Gemini transcripts entirely.** The reader called Claude-only `resolveSessionJsonl`, which walks `projects/<dir>/<id>.jsonl`; a Codex or Gemini session never matched, so it fell back to the index's 300–500-character previews even when its raw transcript was sitting on disk — the whole point of the feature, silently unavailable for every non-Claude session. Those are now located through the session's adapter. The subagent path also discarded its own read-cap count, so an export missing whole agent files still reported `fidelity: "full"` with nothing in the notes; both the per-file and the stopped-early counts now propagate. (#358)
- **Session export: nine review findings, five of them the same silent-loss class the module exists to prevent.** The exporter's own docstring promises that "every drop is either announced inline or counted", and five separate paths broke it: a 20,000-message read cap that stopped with `break` and threw away the number it had stopped at; message counts taken *before* the detail level filtered messages out, so the front matter, subtitle, JSON stats and download toast could all overstate the document; API errors over 400 characters flattened with no marker and no entry in `blocksTruncated`; image attachments dropped from prompts that still reported `fidelity: "full"`; and user entries storing their blocks in top-level `content` — a supported JSONL shape the existing scanner explicitly falls back to — dropped whole. Each now either renders, or is counted and named in the `Export notes` footer. Two more were features that didn't work: `detail=minimal&results=1` resolved to "include tool output, capped at zero characters", so a documented override did nothing; and `sidechains=1` promised subagent content that modern Claude Code no longer stores inline — it lives at `<project>/<session-id>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl`, which the exporter now reads. Assistant messages are also deduplicated by `message.id`/`requestId`, matching `parseSessionTurns`, so a retry or resumed-session re-emit no longer repeats a reply and burns through the read cap. (#358)
- **Toggling a notification channel wiped the settings for the other event.** `PUT /api/config` replaces `notificationPrefs` wholesale rather than merging, but the Settings UI sent only the single key it had changed — so switching a "New manual step" channel on or off silently reset every `awaiting-permission` channel to unset, and vice versa. The client now spreads the sibling events back in. This had been unreachable in practice because `awaiting-permission` had no UI to set it; adding that row (below) is what made an existing bug reachable rather than introducing one.

### Changed
- **Settings → Notifications now exposes the `awaiting-permission` event instead of advertising it as unbuilt.** The section listed three greyed-out rows labelled "Coming in wave 7.2"/"wave 9"; the awaiting-permission dispatcher has in fact been wired for some time, reading `notificationPrefs.events["awaiting-permission"]` that nothing could write. It gets a real channel row. The two rows that remain genuinely unbuilt (session-errored, dispatcher-emergency-stop) are dropped rather than restyled — a notification rule expresses both cases directly (`tool.failed equals true`, or a pattern over `message`), so a fixed row for each would be a narrower version of a feature now shipping alongside it.

## [1.6.1] - 2026-07-22

### Changed
- **`pnpm typecheck` now runs the GA TypeScript 7 native compiler instead of a pinned nightly.** The type-check was pinned to an exact dev build of the preview package (`@typescript/native-preview@7.0.0-dev.20260703.1`); TypeScript 7 has since released, so the checker now runs the GA native (Go) compiler (`7.0.2`). The compiler graduated into the mainline `typescript` package with its binary renamed `tsgo` → `tsc`, while `@typescript/native-preview` stayed a nightly-only channel — so getting onto a stable release meant switching packages, not re-pinning. Because `typescript-eslint` (pulled in transitively by `eslint-config-next`, and lint is a CI gate) still caps its `typescript` peer at `>=4.8.4 <6.1.0`, `typescript@6` is kept for the ESLint/editor/`@types` toolchain and the GA native compiler is added alongside it as an aliased dev dependency (`typescript-native` → `npm:typescript@^7`), invoked by explicit path so it never collides with the `tsc` that `typescript@6` also links. No runtime or shipped-artifact change — the production build already resolved `typescript@6` and is unaffected; this only moves the developer/CI type-check gate onto the released compiler.

### Added
- **`GET /api/projects` now reports project groups — the same repo checked out in more than one location.** A `groups` array pairs checkouts that share a normalized git remote, so a Windows clone and a WSL clone of one repo can be recognized as a single logical project while each location stays independently addressable. Identity is the normalized remote (`host/owner/repo`, case-folded, `.git` and scheme stripped) rather than a slug, because slugs move between rescans when scan roots are reordered. Groups of one are never emitted, which makes "a single-location project behaves exactly as before" structural rather than a convention every consumer has to honour. Deliberately API-only for now — no UI — so the grouping could be validated against real scan data first; that validation is what surfaced the `safe.directory` bug below. Users who want two clones kept genuinely separate can list checkout paths under `ungroupedPaths` in `.minder.json`. Groups will be addressed at `/group/<slug>` when the UI lands, leaving every existing `/project/<slug>` URL meaning exactly what it means today.

### Changed
- **The standalone payload no longer carries the repo's tests, docs, and git history.** Next's file tracer hits a "whole project was traced unintentionally" bailout (#284) and falls back to sweeping the entire repo root into every route's trace — measured at 27,242 entries for `/api/health`, of which 243 were actual `node_modules` dependencies. The underlying bailout is still unfixed; this excludes the directories the server provably never reads at runtime, which bounds the damage to 1,369 entries and cuts traced repo content from 337 MB to 15 MB. `.git/` and `.claude/` are excluded on hygiene grounds rather than size: both were being traced into `.next/standalone` on every build and were stopped only by `package-standalone`'s prune at the copy boundary — the same over-tracing that once shipped a real `.git` and `.env.local` — so they now never enter the pipeline at all. `src/` is deliberately NOT excluded, because the worker resolves `src/lib/db/schema.sql` at runtime. Verified by booting the packaged server from outside the repo: full boot sequence, DB probe `success`, 47 projects scanned, `/api/health` and `/api/projects` both 200.

### Fixed
- **WSL projects had no git data at all — no branch, no remote, no dirty count, no commit history.** Git refuses to operate on a repository whose owner differs from the current user (`fatal: detected dubious ownership`), and every project scanned over UNC — a WSL distro at `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\home\<user>\…` — trips that check, because the files carry a Linux UID. `runGit` catches the error and returns `""`, so the whole `GitInfo` came back `undefined` and the git surface simply rendered blank, with no error anywhere. Minder's own read-only git calls now pass `-c safe.directory=<the directory being read>`, which scopes the waiver to a single invocation and a single repository. That is strictly narrower than the remedy git itself prints in the error (`git config --global --add safe.directory …`, which would also apply to the user's interactive shell and every other tool on the machine), and narrower than a `*` wildcard, which would opt the git process out of the ownership guard for every repository it may traverse — a submodule or nested repo owned by a different user included. Separators are normalized to `/`, because git compares the config value against its own normalized repository path and a `\\wsl.localhost\…` value silently fails to match a repo git reports as `//wsl.localhost/…`. Found while validating project grouping against real scan data: two WSL checkouts were silently unable to group because neither had a remote. Verified end-to-end against the real filesystem — the Windows and WSL checkouts of `bamcli` and `micetrocli` now both resolve `branch=main` and their remote, and group correctly. A test pins the flag's presence and its position before the subcommand, since `-c` is a git-level option and would silently no-op if it were ever moved after it.
- **`ssh://` and `git://` remotes were dropped entirely by the scanner.** Remote normalization handled SCP-style (`git@host:owner/repo`) and http(s) forms, but `ssh://git@host/owner/repo` matched neither branch and fell through to `remoteUrl === undefined` — so a repo cloned that way reported no remote at all, showed no GitHub activity strip, and could not be paired with an https clone of the same repo during project grouping. Both schemes now normalize to the same canonical https form as the others.
- **The bundled Node runtime could ship twice in one installer.** `dist/node` is the ~79 MB Node runtime fetched for the sidecar, and `tauri.conf.json` already bundles it as its own `node` resource — but Next's file tracer also swept it into `.next/standalone`, so `package-standalone` copied it back out to `dist/minder-server/dist/node` and the installer carried two full copies. Release builds escaped this only by accident of step ordering: CI runs `pnpm build` (which traces) before `fetch-node-runtime`, so on a fresh runner the directory doesn't exist yet to be traced. Any repeat build does reproduce it — `pnpm release:local` run twice, or a runner with `dist/` cached — with no error and no signal beyond the installer being larger. `dist/node/**` is now excluded from tracing, so the property holds by construction rather than by ordering. The exclusion is deliberately narrow (`dist/node/`, not `dist/`) because these globs are substring matches and a broad `dist/**` would strip `node_modules/next/dist/**` — the Next runtime itself — out of the payload.
- **The payload hygiene gate now catches that duplication too.** The gate previously matched forbidden *basenames* at any depth, which cannot express this rule: `node` is far too common a directory name to ban outright (`node_modules/.bin/node`, and packages that ship their own `node` subdir). Added a root-anchored rule set alongside it, wired into both consumers the rules module keeps in lockstep — `package-standalone` prunes it at copy time, `verify-payload-hygiene` fails the build if it survives. The wider repo-root over-tracing that puts `src/`, `tests/`, and `docs/` in the payload remains tracked in #284.

## [1.5.2] - 2026-07-20

### Added
- **The running version is now visible in the tray menu and on the Settings page.** Nothing in the UI reported which build was executing, so after an auto-update from 1.5.0 to 1.5.1 there was no way to confirm the update had applied without checking the installer or the Releases page — precisely the question shipping an auto-updater creates. The tray shows a disabled `Version x.y.z` item directly above **Check for updates…**, so the two read together; Settings shows `vx.y.z` under the section nav. The tray reads `app.package_info().version` (from `tauri.conf.json`, stamped from `package.json` at build time by both CI and `pnpm release:local`) rather than a literal, so it reports the binary actually running. Settings reads `version` from `GET /api/health`, which the page already polls every 15s — no new request. The two therefore have independent sources: normally identical, and a disagreement is real information, meaning the tray binary and the bundled server came from different builds.

### Fixed
- **The tray app can no longer report a stale version.** `tauri.conf.json` carried a hardcoded `0.1.0` that CI overwrote from `package.json` at bundle time, so shipped installers were correct but any local `pnpm tray:dev` build displayed `Version 0.1.0` — harmless while nothing rendered the version, and actively misleading the moment the tray menu started showing it. The field is now the relative path `../package.json`, which Tauri resolves natively (documented alternative to a semver literal), making `package.json` the single source of truth for the desktop app, the server, and the release tag alike. Verified by inspecting the generated Windows version resource: `FILEVERSION 1, 5, 2, 0`. The CI stamp step is retained as a redundant safety net, and because it also hard-fails when `package.json` disagrees with the release tag.
- **The Settings DB-status row went blank exactly when the database was broken.** `/api/health` answers 503 for every DB state other than `success` and carries the full body regardless of status code — a contract its own header comment states explicitly, because the Home banner, Settings, and the tray all read the body either way. The Settings query nevertheless threw on a bare `!res.ok`, discarding the payload in precisely the situation the payload exists to describe: `InitStatusRow` renders nothing on a null status, so the DB status, attempt count, quarantine count, and last error all disappeared the moment the DB failed, leaving the page looking healthy. Found while adding the version display, which read the same query and inherited the same blindness. The query now treats 503 as a readable response and still throws on other non-OK statuses.

## [1.5.1] - 2026-07-20

### Fixed
- **`MINDER_STATE_DIR` now relocates the SQLite databases, not just config and caches (#303).** `index.db` and `tasks.db` were pinned to `os.homedir()/.minder` regardless of the variable, so a relocated install still wrote its databases to the real home — and a test run had no way to point them elsewhere. Both now check `MINDER_STATE_DIR` first, falling back to the historical `~/.minder`. Deliberately not routed through `resolveStateDir()`, whose fallback is `process.cwd()`: that would silently relocate every existing user's index to whatever directory they launched from and hand them an empty dashboard. The tray already sets the variable to `~/.minder`, so packaged installs resolve exactly as before.
- **The test suite is no longer affected by a `MINDER_STATE_DIR` inherited from the developer's shell.** Because `DB_DIR` resolves that variable ahead of `os.homedir()`, and ~23 test files isolate their database by spying `os.homedir()`, a machine with the variable set bypassed every one of those spies at once — 24 failures that look like a code regression but are purely environmental, and, more seriously, "isolated" tests then opening and writing the developer's real relocated `index.db`/`tasks.db`. A vitest `setupFile` now deletes the variable before any test module is imported, so the suite behaves identically whether or not it is set. Verified by running the full suite with `MINDER_STATE_DIR` pointed at a scratch directory: 3822 passing and the directory left empty.
- **Flaky `verify-windows`: the SQL schema snapshot's live-DB check raced tests that were still building a database (#330).** The check was gated on `existsSync(DB_PATH)`, which answers "does a file exist" when the question it needs answered is "is there a migrated schema to compare against". On CI the file could exist while another test file was mid-migration, so the block opened a half-built database and read an empty `PRAGMA table_info`, failing on `meta` with `expected [] to deeply equal ["key", "value"]`. It passed on re-run with no code change, and only on Windows, where slower scheduling widens the window. The guard now verifies a populated `meta` table before running, treating a locked, corrupt, or mid-write database as nothing to test rather than as a failure. The test also imports `DB_PATH` instead of recomputing `os.homedir()/.minder/index.db`, which had pinned it to the real home irrespective of isolation.

- **A Claude home outside `/home` — including root's — could not be mapped at all.** `deriveWslMapping` hardcoded the `/home/<user>` shape, so `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\root\.claude` returned no mapping and a root-user WSL setup could never correlate its sessions to scanned projects. The derivation now takes whatever directory contains `.claude` as the home, at any depth (`…\opt\myuser\.claude` → `/opt/myuser`). Surfaced while collapsing the two parallel WSL path parsers introduced in the previous release (#326): `ClaudeHomesSection` and the Scan Roots section each parsed WSL UNC paths independently, which is the "identity derived in more than one place" pattern that produced several defects in that round. They now share one parse, but keep separate prefix inference on purpose — a scan root can sit at any depth so its home must be inferred from convention, while a Claude home names its own directory exactly. Collapsing them onto the root-based rule would have mapped `…\opt\myuser\.claude` to `/opt`, a prefix broad enough to swallow unrelated paths.

### Performance
- **The scan's `.git` pre-filter no longer issues an unbounded burst of stats.** Narrowing a root's directories to git repos before slug assignment ran one `fs.stat` per directory through a single `Promise.all`, bypassing the `BATCH_SIZE` throttle the scan immediately below respects (#328). Mild on a local disk; meaningful on a UNC/WSL root where each stat is a network round-trip — and those are the roots most likely to hold many directories now that multi-root scanning works, so a user who lowered `scanBatchSize` specifically to reduce load still ate the unthrottled burst.
- **The per-project Sessions tab was always empty for WSL projects (#325).** `ProjectSessions` built its session key by string-replacing the project path in the browser (`projectPath.replace(/[:\\/]/g, "-")`), then filtered for an exact match on `SessionSummary.projectName`. That only ever worked because a Windows path happens to encode to the same string Claude Code uses for its `~/.claude/projects/` directory — and it was structurally blind to `config.pathMappings`, which is applied server-side. A WSL project scanned at `\\wsl.localhost\Ubuntu-26.04\home\josh\printing-press\library\bamcli` produced `--wsl.localhost-Ubuntu-26.04-home-josh-…`, while Claude Code, running inside the distro, had recorded those sessions under the Linux path in `-home-josh-printing-press-library-bamcli`. The filter matched nothing and the tab rendered empty — for a project with 8 sessions and 115 MB of history. The component now takes a new scanner-derived `ProjectData.usageDirName` — the encoded conversation-dir name, i.e. exactly `SessionSummary.projectName` — and filters on that, so the match holds by construction rather than by coincidence and the path mapping is applied where it can actually be seen. Deliberately **not** `usageSlug`: that field exists to join usage aggregates and is derived through `toSlug`, which strips the drive prefix, so `C:\dev\foo` and `D:\dev\foo` share the slug `dev-foo` and a slug-keyed filter would show each project the other's sessions — losing the exactness the old `projectName` check did have. Covered by tests asserting the encoded dir resolves the real WSL directory, that the slug genuinely collides across drives while the encoded dir does not, and a negative control pinning why the old browser-built key could not match. The pre-existing limitation that two distros with identical layouts share a `usageSlug` (see `ProjectData.usageHomeKey`) is unchanged.
- **Port-conflict warnings went missing for two checkouts of the same repo.** `detectPortConflicts` accumulated project names in a `Set` keyed by `project.name`, so two clones sharing a `package.json` name collapsed to one entry and no conflict was emitted — losing the warning for precisely the duplicated-checkout case that keeping both projects now enables. Conflicts are keyed by slug (unique) and labelled by name, with the slug appended when two members would otherwise render as the same string.
- **Insights from a disambiguated project were filed under the project that won the plain slug.** `scanInsightsMd` derived its own `toSlug(path.basename(projectPath))` rather than receiving the scanner's resolved slug, and the cross-project insights browser groups and links by that field — so `bamcli-library`'s insights appeared under, and linked to, the unrelated `bamcli`. The resolved slug is now passed in.
- **A project's PR↔session cross-links broke as soon as its slug was disambiguated.** `ProjectDetail` passed `project.slug` to `/api/sessions`, which matches `s.projectSlug` (derived from the encoded conversation dir) and otherwise falls back to a *substring* match on the project name. The route slug only ever matched through that fallback, and `"bamcli".includes("bamcli-library")` is false. It now passes `usageSlug`, built by the same encode→canonicalize→toSlug pipeline as `s.projectSlug` — an exact match rather than a lucky one, and one that applies `pathMappings` so WSL projects resolve too. (The equivalent blindness in `ProjectSessions`, which re-encoded `projectPath` in the browser and so could not see `pathMappings`, was filed as #325 and is fixed in the entry above.)
- **Two WSL distros sharing a Linux home path silently left the second unmapped.** Deriving companions for `\\wsl.localhost\Debian\home\josh` when `/home/josh` was already mapped to an Ubuntu tree produced a duplicate `from` prefix. Since `mapForeignPath` returns on the first matching prefix, a second entry would have been dead config that made the setup *look* fixed while that distro still read as zero — and a `from`-only check would have reported the root as configured. Matching is now on the mapping's `to`, and this case is surfaced as an unresolvable **conflict** (a Linux path carries no distro to tell them apart) with an explanation, instead of a repair button that does nothing.
- **Adding a WSL scan root left it half configured, so its projects reported zero sessions and zero cost forever.** A WSL root needs two companion settings to be useful: a `claudeHomes` entry (sessions recorded inside the distro live in *that distro's* `~/.claude`, a different Claude home — without it those JSONL files are never opened) and a `pathMappings` entry (the distro recorded them under `/home/josh/...` while the scanner sees `\\wsl.localhost\...` — without it `mapLocalPath` is the identity function and the two views of one directory can never be matched). `PATCH /api/config` already validated and persisted both, and `/api/wsl` already *returned suggested values* for them, but `ScanRootsSection.save()` sent only `{ devRoots }` — it fetched `claudeHomes` into state and dropped it, and `pathMappings` wasn't in its type at all. Both failures are silent in the worst way: the projects scan and look healthy, and everything attached to them reads as zero, which is indistinguishable from "I haven't worked there yet". Measured on one real setup, **97% of a project's session data was invisible** (8 sessions / 115 MB in WSL vs 1 / 3.9 MB on Windows). Companions are now **derived from the root path** rather than only from what Detect WSL suggested, so a hand-typed root gets the same treatment as a discovered one — which matters because the roots most likely to be typed by hand are the ones not at the conventional `~/dev`. The mapping is cut at the *user home* rather than the scan root, so a single entry covers every present and future repo under that home, including ones nested well below the root. Existing entries are never overwritten: a hand-tuned mapping (different distro, bind mount, renamed home) is more authoritative than anything derivable from a path, so the merge only ever adds. The derivation preserves whichever UNC host alias the root used, because `mapLocalPath` folds `wsl$`/`wsl.localhost` together but `mapForeignPath` concatenates `to` verbatim — emitting the other alias would produce mapped paths no scanned project matches. Setups configured before this existed have no symptom to notice, so Settings now detects the half-configured state against saved config and offers a one-click **Link WSL Claude data** repair. New `src/lib/wslCompanions.ts` (`deriveWslCompanions` / `mergeWslCompanions` / `analyzeWslRoots`), covered by 31 cases in `tests/wslCompanions.test.ts` — including an end-to-end assertion that the derived mapping resolves a real WSL project to the exact encoded session-directory name Claude Code wrote inside the distro, plus a negative control proving it does *not* resolve without the mapping.
- **A project whose directory name matched one in an earlier scan root vanished from the dashboard entirely.** `scanAllProjects` deduplicated across roots by slug and dropped the loser *before scanning it* — so with `C:\dev` ahead of a WSL root, a `bamcli` checked out in both places existed only as the Windows copy, and the WSL one had no card, no detail page, and no costs. The only evidence was a `console.warn` on the server; nothing surfaced in the UI, which made it read as "WSL scanning is broken" rather than "this specific name is taken". Collisions now **disambiguate instead of dropping**: the first root keeps the plain slug and later roots take a suffix derived from the root's own folder name (`bamcli` + `…\printing-press\library` → `bamcli-library`), falling back to a counter when the root offers no usable hint (a bare drive) or the suffixed form is itself taken. The suffix is derived from the root rather than a bare index so it is stable across rescans and legible in a URL — slugs are identity here, keying `/project/[slug]`, the `.minder.json` `statuses`/`portOverrides` maps, and the `?project=` filter on catalog entries, so a slug that shifted with directory-iteration order would silently move a saved status onto a different project. Resolution had to move *ahead* of the scan rather than renaming afterwards, because `scanProject` stamps the slug onto every skill/agent/command entry as it walks them; a post-hoc rename would have left those catalog entries pointing at the colliding name and merged two projects' catalogs under one filter. For the same reason the `.git` check is now hoisted to the caller, so a non-repo directory cannot consume a disambiguated slug that a real project needs. This also fixes the pre-existing intra-root case where two names normalize to one slug (`bam_cli` and `bam-cli`), which previously produced two projects sharing an identity. Ordering caveat, now documented: which project keeps the undecorated slug still follows root order, so reordering roots in Settings moves the suffix — a strict improvement on the old behaviour, where reordering decided which project *existed*. Carried-forward projects from a skipped root (stopped WSL distro) deliberately keep their stored slug rather than being re-disambiguated, since their cached catalog walks were tagged with it. Covered by 18 cases in the new `tests/slugCollision.test.ts`.

## [1.5.0] - 2026-07-20

### Added
- **The tray app now updates itself (plan task U).** Registers `tauri-plugin-updater` against a `latest.json` published on each GitHub Release, with payloads signed by a project minisign key — an installed client refuses any update it cannot verify. This is **independent of OS code signing**, which is still not done: installers remain unsigned and a first-time download still trips SmartScreen/Gatekeeper. New `crate::updater` runs its periodic check off the *existing* 15s health-poll timer via a tick counter (~6h) rather than adding a second thread, and that periodic path only **notifies** — it never installs, because this app supervises a dashboard server the user may be actively using and an unannounced restart would drop in-flight scans and any managed dev servers. Installing is an explicit "Check for updates…" tray item. The Windows install hand-off is the load-bearing part: `download_and_install` quits the app via `std::process::exit()`, which (a) never reaches the event loop, so `main.rs`'s `prevent_exit` guard cannot swallow it — resolving the U4 risk in our favour — and (b) runs no destructors, so the Node sidecar would survive as an orphan holding a handle on `resources/node/node.exe` and fail the install on a locked file; `supervisor.shutdown()` therefore runs from the plugin's `on_before_exit` hook, the only code that executes in that window. macOS/Linux are not force-exited and stop the sidecar then `app.restart()` explicitly. `Outcome` deliberately has no `Installed` variant: a successful install never returns on any platform. An in-flight flag prevents a menu click during a periodic download from starting a second ~100 MB transfer, and async runs on `tauri::async_runtime` (no new dependency); `rust-version` 1.77 → 1.77.2 for the plugin's MSRV. CI gains a `updater-manifest` aggregation job that assembles `latest.json` from one signature fragment per build job — necessarily a separate job, since the manifest names all four platforms at once — via a new testable `scripts/updater-manifest.mjs` (`emit` per job, `merge` at the end) rather than inline YAML. The merge **throws rather than emitting a partial manifest**, because Tauri validates the whole document before comparing versions, so one malformed entry breaks update checks for every user on every platform. macOS arm64 now builds `dmg,app` (the updater can only consume a `.app.tar.gz`; without it Apple Silicon users would install fine and never update), and the hand-rolled Intel `.app` tarball is gone — Tauri's own signed updater tarball serves as that job's single download, so the copy users get is the signed one. Asset names are percent-encoded (they contain spaces) and the arch-less macOS tarball is renamed per-arch so the two Mac jobs cannot clobber each other. `.deb`/`.rpm` structurally cannot self-update ("Currently only an AppImage can be updated") and this is documented rather than worked around. 7 Rust tests + 21 cases in the new `tests/updaterManifest.test.ts`; documented in `docs/help/tray-app.md`.
- **`pnpm release:local` reproduces the full installer build on a developer machine (plan task R1).** The release chain existed only as five separate steps inside `.github/workflows/release-installers.yml`, so building an installer locally meant running them by hand in the right order — and getting it wrong produced a subtly broken payload rather than an error, most dangerously by skipping the payload hygiene gate that keeps `.git`/`.env*`/`.claude/` out of a shipped bundle (#284). New `scripts/release-local.mjs` runs the same five steps in the same order (`pnpm build` → `pnpm package:standalone` → hygiene gate → pinned Node fetch → `pnpm tauri build`), aborting on the first non-zero exit, and prints the resulting artifact paths and sizes — treating "built successfully but produced no installer" as a failure, matching CI's `if-no-files-found: error`. It performs the **same `tauri.conf.json` version stamp** CI does (`package.json` is the single source of truth) and then restores the file, so it leaves no working-tree diff; the stamp is load-bearing rather than cosmetic, because an unstamped build reports itself as the placeholder `0.1.0` forever and, once the updater lands, would consider itself permanently ancient and re-download every release in a loop. When `HEAD` carries a `v*` tag the script applies CI's mistagged-release guard (tag must equal `package.json`); on an untagged commit it says so and proceeds, since building a release candidate before tagging is the normal local workflow — a hard tag requirement would block the script's main use. `--skip-build` / `--skip-node` allow iterating on the Tauri layer without repeating the slow earlier steps (the Node fetch alone is ~80 MB), and both fail with a specific message when the artifact they assume is missing; the hygiene gate is deliberately **not** skippable. `--bundles` defaults to the host OS's natural target set (Intel macOS omits `dmg`, mirroring the CI matrix where `hdiutil detach` times out deterministically) and is validated against a strict lowercase-alphanumeric allowlist, because on Windows `pnpm` is a `.cmd` that Node refuses to spawn without `shell: true`, putting the value on a shell command line. Pure decision logic lives in `scripts/release/lib.mjs` (same split as `service.mjs` + `service/lib.mjs`), covered by 30 cases in the new `tests/releaseLocal.test.ts`. Documented in `docs/help/tray-app.md`. Installers remain **unsigned**.
- **First-run setup for installs on a machine with no conventional projects folder.** `getDefaultDevRoot()` returned a hardcoded `C:\dev` on Windows (while correctly using `os.homedir()` on POSIX), so a fresh install on any Windows machine without that exact folder scanned a directory that wasn't there and rendered an empty dashboard indistinguishable from a broken one — the single worst first impression in the installer story now that the tray ships the whole app. Dev-root resolution is now a probe over an ordered candidate list (new `getDevRootCandidates()`: `C:\dev` then `~/dev` on Windows, `~/dev` on POSIX — `C:\dev` still leads so existing installs resolve exactly as before): `probeDefaultDevRoot()` returns the first candidate that EXISTS, or `null` when none do, and an unreadable candidate (permissions, disconnected drive) is treated as absent rather than throwing. `DEFAULT_DEV_ROOT` now prefers a candidate that exists over the bare first choice, so a machine with `~/dev` but no `C:\dev` scans what it actually has. `null` is the first-run signal: new `isFirstRun()` reports true only when **both** no `.minder.json` exists on disk **and** no candidate root exists — checking the FILE, not `readConfig()` (which always succeeds by falling back to defaults and so cannot distinguish "no config" from "default config"), so anyone who has saved configuration once is never dropped back into setup even if their roots are temporarily unreachable (unplugged drive, stopped WSL distro). A configured-but-empty root is deliberately NOT first-run — that's a legitimate steady state. New `GET /api/first-run` returns `{ firstRun, candidates }` as its own route rather than a field on `GET /api/config`, because that route returns the bare `MinderConfig` and clients round-trip it through `PUT` — a derived field there would be persisted into `.minder.json` on the next save. New `FirstRunSetup` component replaces the dashboard (not a banner over an empty grid) when the flag is set, naming the paths that were probed and saving via the existing `PATCH /api/config { devRoots }` followed by an explicit `POST /api/scan` — without that rescan the user would stare at an empty grid until the 5-minute scan-cache TTL lapsed and conclude setup had failed. There is intentionally no folder-picker button: browsers cannot hand a server-side process an absolute filesystem path (`showDirectoryPicker` yields a sandboxed handle; `<input webkitdirectory>` yields relative paths), and the scanner needs a real one. A failed probe falls through to the normal dashboard so a broken check can never trap an existing user in setup. `docs/help/getting-started.md` documents first run and custom ports, and no longer hardcodes `C:\dev` throughout. Covered by 7 new `probeDefaultDevRoot`/`getDevRootCandidates` cases in `tests/platform.test.ts` and a new `tests/firstRun.test.ts`.

### Fixed
- **The MCP server no longer rejects every request when Minder runs on a non-4100 port.** `src/lib/mcp/server.ts` pinned its transport DNS-rebinding allowlist to the literal `localhost:4100`/`127.0.0.1:4100`, while `src/proxy.ts` had already been made port-aware for the C1 tray (which spawns the packaged server with `PORT=<MINDER_TRAY_PORT>`). A user whose 4100 was taken therefore got a fully working dashboard whose every `/api/mcp` call failed transport validation — Claude Desktop/Code simply couldn't connect, with no dashboard-visible symptom. Both allowlists now derive from a single new `src/lib/boundPort.ts` (`resolveBoundPort` / `buildAllowedHosts` / `buildAllowedOrigins`), which `proxy.ts` re-exports for its existing tests; the MCP list also gains the `[::1]` entry the proxy already had. The security property is preserved verbatim: **only** the bound port is trusted, never the canonical 4100 as well (trusting :4100 on a server bound to 4199 would let any other local process serving a page on :4100 drive the 4199 APIs cross-origin — issue #283). Covered by new `tests/boundPort.test.ts` (18 cases, including a lockstep guard asserting the host and origin sets can never diverge again).
- **`pnpm service:install` on a custom port produced a service `pnpm service:stop` could not stop.** The generated Windows Scheduled Task VBS, macOS LaunchAgent plist, and Linux systemd unit all baked a literal `PORT=4100`, and `service:stop` scanned that same literal for a LISTENING PID. Install-time port selection is now `resolveServicePort()` (`MINDER_PORT` preferred over `PORT` so the service's port is independent of whatever else in the shell inherits `PORT`), and — critically — **stop no longer re-resolves the port from the environment**.
